Existence of suitable weak solutions to an anisotropic electrokinetic flow model
Dietmar Hömberg, Robert Lasarzik, Luisa Plato

Abstract
In this article we present a system of coupled non-linear PDEs modeling an anisotropic electrokinetic flow. We show the existence of suitable weak solutions in three spatial dimensions, that is weak solutions which fulfill an energy inequality, via a regularized system. The flow is modeled by a Navier–Stokes–Nernst–Planck–Poisson system and the anisotropy is introduced via space dependent diffusion matrices in the Nernst–Planck and Poisson equations.

1
Introduction

Electrokinetics is essential in the design of nano-fluid “lab-on-a-chip” devices
[
62
]
. These devices require the mixing of tiny fluid volumes, but at such small scales, mechanical stirring becomes impractical due to, amongst other reasons, the high viscosity of the fluid. To address this challenge, one innovative approach involves dissolving ions in the fluid and using an electric field to manipulate the flow for effective mixing
[
62
]
.

When charged particles
c^{\pm}
are dissolved in an incompressible fluid with velocity
\boldsymbol{v}
and under the influence of an external electric field
-\nabla\psi
,
three major effects govern the movement of the charges.
The charges diffuse, they are transported by the surrounding fluid and the electric field induces a directed movement, called electromigration.
Further, space charge exerts an electric body force on the fluid velocity.
An attempt to model this physical interaction leads us to a coupled Navier–Stokes–Nernst–Planck–Poisson (NSNPP) system,
[
47
, Chap. 3.4]
.
More explicitly, we are considering,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Re}\left(\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}\right)-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}+\nabla p+\mathbb{\alpha}(c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(1a)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(1b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pe}\left(\partial_{t}c^{\pm}+\nabla\cdot(c^{\pm}\boldsymbol{v})\right)-\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm\mathbb{\beta}c^{\pm}\nabla\psi)\right)

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(1c)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)-\mathbb{\gamma}(c^{+}-c^{-})

\displaystyle=0\quad\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(1d)

where
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a smooth and bounded domain,
\mathrm{Re},\mathrm{Pe},\mathbb{\alpha},\mathbb{\beta},\mathbb{\gamma},\lambda,\varepsilon>0

are positive constants,

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d}):=\boldsymbol{I}+\varepsilon\,\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d}):=\boldsymbol{I}+\lambda\,\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}
,
where
\boldsymbol{d}
is the so-called director
with
\boldsymbol{d}(\boldsymbol{x})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
.
The evolution of the fluid’s velocity field is described by the Navier–Stokes equations for incompressible fluids (
1a
)–(
1b
).
The charge densities evolve according to the Nernst–Planck equations (
1c
) including a diffusion term
-\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla c^{\pm})
as well as two transport terms, one due to the velocity field
\boldsymbol{v}
and one due to the electric field
-\nabla\psi
.
The notation
c^{\pm}
means an enumeration, so that (
1c
) in fact denotes two equations: one for the positive charges
c^{+}
with a plus in front of the
\nabla\psi
term and one for the negative charges
c^{-}
with a minus in front of the
\nabla\psi
.
Finally, the electric potential is given by the Poisson equation (
1d
).

In isotropic fluids the movement of the charges and the formation of the electric field are independent of the direction.
Not so in anisotropic fluids, where the movement of the ions due to diffusion and electromigration, as well as the diffusion of the electric potential
may depend on the direction.
In our case they depend on the director
\boldsymbol{d}
, which gives the preferred direction of motion.
Our choice
for the matrices
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})
stems
from the modeling of liquid crystals, most famously known for their application in LCDs.
One possible way to model liquid crystals are the Erickson–Leslie equations
[
21
]
,
including a time-evolution for the director
\boldsymbol{d}
.
The mobilities of the charges vary depending on the motion being parallel or perpendicular to the director,
see
[
9
]
for an extensive model derivation for nematic electrolytes.
As it was done in
[
9
]
we choose the anisotropy matrices to be of the form
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})
.
For simplicity, we assume that
\varepsilon>0
and
\lambda>0
. There are certain materials for which
\varepsilon
and
\lambda
may be negative or even have different signs.
Typically, the conductivity and thus the mobility of the ions is larger parallel to the directors
[
13
, Chap. 5.3]
.
This justifies the assumption
\lambda>0
.
For
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq 1
, we could also treat the case
\varepsilon>-1
, but refrain from this technical improvement, since it would further impede the estimates.

Additionally, the system is equipped with the following boundary conditions

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}=0\;\text{ and }\;\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\left(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm\mathbb{\beta}c^{\pm}\nabla\psi\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0\text{ on }\Gamma\times[0,T],

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\psi=\xi\text{ on }\Gamma\times[0,T],

where
\tau
is a positive constant and

\xi\in W^{1,2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Gamma))
the externally applied electric potential.
These are the standard no-slip boundary conditions for the velocity field, no-flux boundary condition for the charge densities,
which correspond to the assumption that the charges cannot cross the boundary of the domain and Robin boundary conditions for the electric potential.
As in
[
7
]
, we choose Robin boundary conditions
to model the electric double layer, which usually forms at an electrolyte-solid-interface
[
42
, Chap. 7]
.

As described in
[
9
]
anisotropic fluids, like liquid crystals, have certain advantages to isotropic fluids in producing persistent flows
under the influence of an alternating electric field.
In experiments it is possible to fix the director of the liquid crystal such that an alternating field creates vortices in the fluid which enhance mixing
[
45
]
.
This was our motivation to consider the above system (
1
).
Our numerical simulations of system (
1
) give qualitatively similar result to the observed flows in the experiments in
[
45
]
.
The Python code used for the simulations is published on Zenodo
[
46
]
.
For example
[
45
, Fig. 4]
shows a given director field with the observed velocity field.
Using this director field, see Figure
1(a)
, our simulation of the velocity field,
cf.
Figure
1(b)
, is in good agreement with the observed one
[
45
, Fig. 4]
.
Figure
1(b)
shows a tracer plot of the time-averaged velocity field.

(a)

Director field.

(b)

Simulated velocity field.

Figure 1
:
Simulated velocity field for a given director field.

The simulation was performed via a finite element discretization using the Python package FEniCS
[
2
,
39
]

and the figures were created with the visualization tool ParaView
[
4
]
.

In case of
c^{\pm}=0=\nabla\psi
, system (
1
) reduces to the well-known Navier–Stokes equations
[
24
]
.
In his seminal work, Jean Leray showed the existence of weak solutions for the Navier–Stokes equation in three space dimensions,
which he termed “turbulent solutions”
[
37
]
.
In contrast to the two dimensional case, in three space dimensions these solutions may not be unique
[
1
]
,
but under additional smoothness assumption uniqueness can be shown, as it was already done by Leray himself
[
37
]
and by
James Serrin in 1962
[
54
]
.
As the Navier–Stokes equations are a sub-problem of system (
1
) we do not hope to attain uniqueness of our solutions,
but instead show weak-strong uniqueness, which we will be done in a subsequent article.
The situation for the Nernst–Planck–Poisson equations is somewhat similar in the sense,
that uniquness is known in dimension two but remains an open probem in dimension three. In
[
10
]
the stability of steady-state solutions was proven in two dimensions
and conditionally under the assumption of an
L^{\infty}(L^{2})-
estimate for the ions in dimension three.
Today, the existence of weak solutions is well understood in all space dimensions.
In
[
6
]
, the existence of weak solutions for a Nernst–Planck–Poisson system with multiple ion species
with diffusion coefficients variable in time and space and bounded reactions is shown in all space dimension.
Here again the question of uniqueness remains an open problem
[
6
]
.

The coupled NSNPP was introduced by Rubinstein in 1990
[
51
]

and there is a myriad of works on this system with scalar diffusion coefficient.
One of the first mathematically rigorous investigations of the system was performed in
[
32
]
,
where the existence of local strong solutions to an NSNPP system with two ion species with constant scalar diffusion was proven via a semigroup approach.
In
[
7
]
, the local existence of strong solutions as well as the global existence of strong solutions in dimension two is proven
via maximal
L^{p}-
regularity for multiple ion species.
The two dimensional case was also considered in
[
11
]
, where the existence of global strong solutions as well as convergence to a steady-state was proven
for non-homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions for the ions.
In
[
53
]
, the existence and uniqueness of weak solutions to an NSNPP system for two ion species under no-flux boundary conditions for the ions was proven
in dimension two.
In
[
12
]
, an NSNPP as well as a Stokes–Nernst–Planck–Poisson (SNPP) system was considered with Dirichlet boundary conditions for the electric potential and the local existence of strong solutions was proven.
Additionally, the global existence of strong solutions for arbitrary many ion species with identical diffusion coefficients and for two ion species with possibly different diffusion coefficient was proven for the SNPP system and,
under additional regularity assumptions on
the velocity field, also for the NSNPP system.
A similar result for Robin boundary conditions for the electric potential was obtained in
[
35
]
.

All of the above mentioned results are on systems with scalar diffusion coefficients.
An anisotropic Nernst–Planck–Poisson system similar to (
1c
)–(
1d
) coupled to Erickson–Leslie equations, modeling nematic liquid crystals, was considered in

[
5
]
. Here, the existence of dissipative solutions was proven, but the proof relied on the fact that the anisotropy matrix
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})
in the
Nernst–Planck equations (
1c
) and the anisotropy matrix
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})
in the Poisson equation (
1d
) are identical.
This property was also used in the weak-sequential stability proof in
[
26
]
.

The inclusion of diffusion matrices in the Nernst–Planck and Poisson equations is indispensable to handle anisotropy
and to the best of the authors’ such a general NSNPP system with diffusion matrices has not yet been considered.
We show that this anisotropic electrokinetic flow model admits a weak solution.
The difference between the anisotropy matrices
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})
and
\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})
is crucial for capturing the distinct physical properties of electric conductivity and electric permittivity,
which are inherently different physical properties.
Furthermore, we establish that there are weak solutions that satisfy an energy-dissipation inequality.
Energy dissipation is not only a physically relevant property but it is also essential for proving relative energy inequalities,
which can be used to prove the weak-strong uniqueness of such solutions.
This means that if a strong solution exists, any suitable weak solution emanating from the same initial data will coincide with that strong solution, ensuring its uniqueness within the class of suitable weak solutions.

We adapt the
ansatz
from
[
27
]
to show the existence of suitable weak solutions.
Due to the differences in the anisotropy matrices new
a priori
estimates
for the first-order derivatives of the charges and the second-order derivatives of the electric potential are required.
These can be accomplished through integration by parts, necessitating careful control of the boundary integrals.
This control is achieved using surface differential operators and performing integration by parts on the boundary.

The article is organized as follows. First, in the following section, Section
2
, we introduce some notation and state our main result.
Then, we recall some known results for our chosen regularization operators and Sobolev functions on the boundary of a domain, see Section
3
.
In Section
4
we give the proof of the main result and auxiliary results are collected in the Appendix.

2
Main results

We begin by introducing some basic notation.
By
\Omega
, we denote a smooth bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
.
For all
r\in(1,\infty)
we define the function spaces

L^{r}_{\sigma}(\Omega):=\overline{C^{\infty}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega)}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{L^{r}}}\quad\text{ and }\quad W_{0}^{1,r}(\Omega)=\overline{C^{\infty}_{0}(\Omega)}^{\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{W^{1,r}}}.

The function space
L^{p}(\Omega)_{+}
is defined via
L^{p}(\Omega)_{+}:=\{f\in L^{p}(\Omega)\mid f\geq 0\text{ a.e.\penalty\ in }\Omega\}
.
The norm
|\cdot|_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})}
is defined via
|\boldsymbol{a}|_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})}^{2}=|\boldsymbol{a}|^{2}+\lambda(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{a})^{2}
and similarly for
|\cdot|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}
.
The outer normal of
\Omega
is denoted by
\boldsymbol{n}
.
The standard matrix and matrix-vector multiplication is written without an extra sign for brevity,

\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{B}=\left[\sum_{j=1}^{3}\boldsymbol{A}_{ij}\boldsymbol{B}_{jk}\right]_{i,k=1}^{3}\,,\quad\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{a}=\left[\sum_{j=1}^{3}\boldsymbol{A}_{ij}\boldsymbol{a}_{j}\right]_{i=1}^{3}\,,\quad\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},\,\boldsymbol{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3},\,\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

The outer vector product is given by

\boldsymbol{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}:=\boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}^{T}=\left[\boldsymbol{a}_{i}\boldsymbol{b}_{j}\right]_{i,j=1}^{3}
for two vectors
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and by
\boldsymbol{A}\otimes\boldsymbol{a}:=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{a}^{T}=\left[\boldsymbol{A}_{ij}\boldsymbol{a}_{k}\right]_{i,j,k=1}^{3}
for a matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
and a vector
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
We use the Nabla symbol
\nabla
for real-valued functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
, vector-valued functions
\boldsymbol{f}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
as well as matrix-valued functions
\boldsymbol{A}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
denoting

\displaystyle\nabla f:=\left[\frac{\partial f}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}_{i}}\right]_{i=1}^{3}\,,\quad\nabla\boldsymbol{f}:=\left[\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{f}_{i}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}_{j}}\right]_{i,j=1}^{3}\,,\quad\nabla\boldsymbol{A}:=\left[\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{A}_{ij}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}_{k}}\right]_{i,j,k=1}^{3}\,.

The divergence of a vector-valued and a matrix-valued function is defined by

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{f}:=\sum_{i=1}^{3}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{f}_{i}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}_{i}}\,,\quad\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{A}:=\left[\sum_{j=1}^{3}\frac{\partial\boldsymbol{A}_{ij}}{\partial\boldsymbol{x}_{j}}\right]_{i=1}^{3}\,.

These definitions give rise to different calculation rules,
e.g.
\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{b})=\nabla\boldsymbol{a}\boldsymbol{b}+\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{b}\boldsymbol{a}
for
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in W^{1,2}(\Omega)
.
The use of
\displaystyle\,\cdot\,
\scriptscriptstyle\pm
implies a summation: for example
\;\hbox to36.26pt{\vbox to14.26pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 14.89513pt\lower-6.47043pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{

, and

\;\hbox to66.22pt{\vbox to15.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.87842pt\lower-7.30376pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt=c^{+}(\ln c^{+}+1)+c^{-}(\ln c^{-}+1).

So the sum
\displaystyle\,\cdot\,
\scriptscriptstyle\pm
consists of two terms: one where only the upper signs of
\pm
and
\mp
are used, and one where only the lower signs are used.
Outside of these brackets
\pm
is used as an enumeration,
e.g.
c^{\pm}\in L^{1}(\Omega)
means
c^{+}\in L^{1}(\Omega)
and
c^{-}\in L^{1}(\Omega)
.

For a given Banach space
V
, the space
C_{w}([0,T];V)
denotes the functions on
[0,T]
taking values in
V
that are continuous with respect to the weak topology of
V
.

In the case
\Gamma\in C^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
we denote for
p\in(1,\infty)
by

S:W^{m,p}(\Omega)\to W^{m-\frac{1}{p},p}(\Gamma)
,
the usual trace operator and by

E:W^{m-\frac{1}{p},p}(\Gamma)\to W^{m,p}(\Omega)
its right-inverse such that

S(E(f))=f
for all
f\in W^{m-\frac{1}{p},p}(\Gamma)
.

Throughout this work
C>0
denotes a generic constant, which may change its value without an indication in the notation.
We sometimes use
C(\cdot)
to indicate dependencies of this constant.

With these notations ar hand we come back to our PDE model and give its precise formulation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}-\Delta\boldsymbol{v}+\nabla p+(c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi

\displaystyle=0,\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(2a)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=0,\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(2b)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c^{\pm}+\nabla\cdot(c^{\pm}\boldsymbol{v})-\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm c^{\pm}\nabla\psi)\right)

\displaystyle=0,\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(2c)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)-(c^{+}-c^{-})

\displaystyle=0,\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(2d)

equipped with initial and the following boundary conditions

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}=0\;\text{ and }\;\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\left(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm c^{\pm}\nabla\psi\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0\text{ on }\Gamma\times[0,T],

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\psi=\xi\text{ on }\Gamma\times[0,T].

Our proof works for arbitrary positive constants
\mathrm{Re},\mathrm{Pe},\mathbb{\alpha},\mathbb{\beta}
and
\mathbb{\gamma}
in (
1
)
but as these have no major impact on our method of proof we set them all to one for now to improve readability.

Assumption 2.1
.

We require that
\Omega
is a smooth domain in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with boundary
\Gamma:=\partial\Omega
, the constants
\lambda
,
\varepsilon
, and
\tau
are positive.
The director field
\boldsymbol{d}\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{3})
fulfills
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
,
and the externally applied electric field
\xi\in W^{1,2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Gamma))
.

Using the same regularization as in
[
27
]
we can prove the existence of suitable weak solutions,
where we define suitable weak solutions as follows.

Definition 2.2

(Suitable weak solutions)
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded, smooth domain,
T>0
,

(\boldsymbol{v}_{0},c^{\pm}_{0})\in L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)_{+}
,

\boldsymbol{d}\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
and
\xi\in W^{1,2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Gamma))
.
We call
(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)
suitable weak solution if

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle\in C_{w}([0,T];L^{2}_{\sigma}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W_{0}^{1,2}(\Omega))\cap L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T)),

\displaystyle c^{\pm}

\displaystyle\in C_{w}([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega)_{+})\cap L^{5/4}(0,T;W^{1,5/4}(\Omega))\cap L^{5/3}(\Omega\times(0,T)),

\displaystyle\sqrt{c^{\pm}}

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega)),

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\in C_{w}([0,T];W^{1,2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Omega)),

\displaystyle\sqrt{c^{\pm}}\nabla\psi

\displaystyle\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)),

and (
2
) is fulfilled in the weak sense,
that is for all test functions
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\in L^{2}(0,T,W^{1,2}_{0,\sigma}(\Omega))\cap C^{1}(\overline{\Omega}\times[0,T])
,

\tilde{c}^{\pm}\in C^{1}(\overline{\Omega}\times[0,T])

and
\tilde{\psi}\in W^{1,2}(\Omega)\cap L^{\infty}(\Omega)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}})(t)-\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(0)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\boldsymbol{v}:\nabla\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}-\boldsymbol{v}\partial_{t}\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}+\left((\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}+(c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi\right)\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s

\displaystyle=0

(3)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(c^{\pm}\;\tilde{c}^{\pm})(t)-c^{\pm}_{0}\tilde{c}^{\pm}(0)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm c^{\pm}\nabla\psi)-c^{\pm}\boldsymbol{v}\right)\cdot\nabla\tilde{c}^{\pm}-c^{\pm}\partial_{t}\tilde{c}^{\pm}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s

\displaystyle=0

(4)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\tilde{\psi}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}(\tau\psi-\xi)\tilde{\psi}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma-\int_{\Omega}(c^{+}-c^{-})\tilde{\psi}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=0

(5)

for all
t\in[0,T]
and additionally the energy inequality

\left[\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}+\;\hbox to66.22pt{\vbox to15.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.87842pt\lower-7.30376pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\leq\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\psi\partial_{t}\xi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s

(6)

holds for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Theorem 2.3

(Existence of suitable weak solutions)
.

Let Assumption
2.1
be fulfilled.
For for every
T>0
and all initial data
(\boldsymbol{v}_{0},c^{\pm}_{0})\in L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)_{+}
there exists a suitable weak
solution according to Definition
2.2
.

Remark 2.4
.

We include the energy inequality in the definition of a suitable solution since it is an important property of a solution.
It is not only a property a solution should fulfill from a point of view of physics, but it also helps to prove the so-called relative energy inequality,
which we will consider in a subsequent article.
The relative energy inequality is an important tool in the analysis of nonlinear evolution equations. As an easy consequence, it provides the weak-strong uniqueness of solutions, i.e., all weak solutions coincide with a local strong solution emanating from the same initial value as long as the latter exists.
Moreover, the relative energy inequality has been used to derive singular limits
[
25
]
, a posteriori error estimates
[
28
]
, convergence of numerical approximations
[
5
]
, or optimal control
[
33
]
for an associated solution concept
[
34
]
.

Remark 2.5
.

The main novelty of this result is that we can include an anisotropy in the system, the prescribed director field
\boldsymbol{d}
. The main technical difficulty is to prove a priori bounds that are strong enough to deduce via compactness results strong converging sequences of our approximate scheme in order to identify the limit as a weak solution in the sense of Definition
2.2
. The approximate scheme follows the ideas of
[
27
]
, which we extend to the considered setting.

We do not consider the cases
\tau=0
,
\lambda=0
, or
\varepsilon=0
, but these cases are significantly easier than the considered case. The cases
\lambda=0
or
\varepsilon=0
could be dealt with by combining our proof with the one conducted in
[
27
]
. For
\tau=0
the system is equipped with inhomogeneous Neumann boundary conditions. In this case, the electric potential is only determined up to a constant and this constant should be fixed a priori.

3
Preliminaries

In order show the existence of weak solutions we regularize our system and let the regularization coefficient
\kappa
tend to zero.
We use elliptic regularization operators of the form
(I-\kappa A)^{-1}
, where
A
is a generator of a bounded
C_{0}-
semigroup.
For the convenience of the reader we first recall some basic properties of operators of that form, which hold true for all generators
and then we recall results, showing that our chosen operatos, namely the anisotropic Robin Laplacian, the Stokes operator,
and the root of the Stokes operator indeed generate bounded
C_{0}-
semigroups.

3.1
Basic properties of the regularization operator

Lemma 3.1

(Properties of the regularization operator)
.

Let
X
be a Banach space and
A:D(A)\subseteq X\to X
the infinitesimal generator of a bounded
C_{0}-
semigroup.
For all
\kappa>0
the operator
R_{\kappa}:=(I-\kappa A)^{-1}:X\to D(A)\subseteq X
is a well-defined linear and bounded operator
with the following properties

1

For
\kappa\searrow 0
and
x_{\kappa}\to x
in
X
we have
R_{\kappa}(x_{\kappa})\to x
in
X
.

2

For
\kappa\searrow 0
and
x_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup x
in
X
we have
R_{\kappa}(x_{\kappa})\rightharpoonup x
in
X
.

3

There exists
C>0
independent of
\kappa
such that
\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x)\right\rVert_{X}\leq C\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{X}
.

4

For any Banach space
Y
such that
D(A)\hookrightarrow Y
there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
\kappa

such that for all
x\in X
we have
\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x)\right\rVert_{Y}\leq C(1+1/\kappa)\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{X}
.

The proof is a rather straight forward and relies on the uniform bound on the operator norm of the resolvent given in the Hille–Yosida generation theorem,

cf.
[
22
, Thm. 3.5]
.
We deferred the proof into the Appendix, see Section
A.3

3.2
The Stokes operator as a generator

Definition 3.2

(Stokes operator)
.

We define the Stokes operator
A_{r}
with domain

D(A_{r}):=W^{2,r}(\Omega)\cap W_{0}^{1,r}(\Omega)\cap L^{r}_{\sigma}(\Omega)

as an unbounded operator in
L_{\sigma}^{r}(\Omega)
via

A_{r}:D(A_{r})\subseteq L^{r}_{\sigma}(\Omega)\to L^{r}_{\sigma}(\Omega),\quad v\mapsto-P_{r}(\Delta v),

where
P_{r}
is the
L^{r}-
Helmholtz projection, cf.
[
55
, Thm. 1.4]
.

We collect several standard properties of the stokes operator

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
r\in(0,\infty)
, we infer

1

For all
r\in(1,\infty)
the Stokes operator
-A_{r}
generates a bounded analytic
C_{0}-
semigroup on
L^{r}_{\sigma}(\Omega)
,
denoted by
\left(\e^{-tA_{r}}\right)_{t\geq 0}
.

2

For all
\alpha\geq 0
the fractional power Stokes operator
-A_{r}^{\alpha}
also generates a bounded analytic
C_{0}-
semigroup

\left(\e^{-tA^{\alpha}_{r}}\right)_{t\geq 0}
in
L^{r}_{\sigma}(\Omega)
.

3

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\in\mathbb{N}
, be a bounded domain with smooth boundary then

D(A_{r}^{1/2})=W_{0}^{1,r}(\Omega)\cap L^{r}_{\sigma}(\Omega)
.

4

The operator
A^{1/2}_{2}
is symmetric.

Proof.

These reults are classical. The first two points can be found in
[
29
, Thm. 2]
and
[
30
, Prop. 1.2]
.
The last two points can be found in
[
58
, Lem. 2.2.1]
.
∎

3.3
The Robin Laplacian as a generator

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\boldsymbol{d}\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
, we define the anisotropic Robin Laplacian
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}
in
L^{2}(\Omega)
by

\displaystyle\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}:D(\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})})

\displaystyle:=\left\{\varphi\in W^{2,2}(\Omega)\,\lvert\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\varphi=0\text{ on }\Gamma\right\}\subseteq L^{2}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega),

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\mapsto\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\varphi\right).

The following result is quite standard and can for example be found in
[
40
, Thm. 3.1.3]
.

Lemma 3.5

(Robin semigroup in
L^{2}(\Omega)
)
.

The anisotropic Robin Laplacian from Definition
3.4
generates a contraction
C_{0}-
semigroup in
L^{2}(\Omega)
.

Remark 3.6
.

The constant
C
from Lemma
3.1
item
3
and item
4

with
A=\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}
can be chosen independently of
\boldsymbol{d}
.
This simply follows from the fact that
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}
generates a
C_{0}-
semigroup of contractions for all
\boldsymbol{d}\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
.

Following the proof of
[
8
, Thm. 8]
one can show the following Lemma,
which is not new but we could not find the result handling our boundary conditions in the literature.
The proof of
[
8
, Thm. 8]
where the result was shown for Dirichlet boundary conditions instead of Robin boundary conditions,
works completely analogous with only minor adaptions to accommodate our boundary conditions.

Lemma 3.7

(Robin semigroup in
L^{1}(\Omega)
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{d}\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
. We define the Robin Laplacian
A_{1}=\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
with domain

D(A_{1})=\left\{\varphi\in W^{1,1}(\Omega)\,\lvert\,A_{1}\varphi\in L^{1}(\Omega)\right\},

where the Laplacian is defined in the sense of distribution, that is we say
A_{1}\varphi=f
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
, if

-\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla w\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}-\tau\int_{\Gamma}\varphi w\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=\int_{\Omega}fw\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}

holds for all
w\in W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)
.
Then
A_{1}
generates a contracting
C_{0}-
semigroup
and we have the embedding
D(A_{1})\hookrightarrow W^{1,q}(\Omega)
for all
q\in[1,3/2)

and for all
\varphi\in D(A_{1})
it holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{W^{1,q}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\lVert A_{1}\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)},

(7)

where the constant
C>0
may depend on the domain
\Omega
but is independent of the director
\boldsymbol{d}
.

Corollary 3.8

(Elliptic
L^{1}-
estimate)
.

Let
\kappa>0
and
f\in L^{1}(\Omega)
be arbitrary,
then

\left(I-\kappa A_{1}\right)^{-1}:L^{1}(\Omega)\to D(A_{1})\subseteq L^{1}(\Omega)

is a well-defined, bounded, and linear operator and it holds

\left\lVert\left(I-\kappa A_{1}\right)^{-1}f\right\rVert_{W^{1,q}(\Omega)}\leq C\frac{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}}{\kappa}

for
C>0
independent of
\kappa
and
\boldsymbol{d}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.7
we have that

A_{1}
generates a contraction
C_{0}-
semigroup
and thus the well-definedness follows from Lemma
3.1
.
For the norm inequality we use the norm inequality (
7
) from Lemma
3.7
above,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(I-\kappa A_{1}\right)^{-1}f\right\rVert_{W^{1,q}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert A_{1}\left((I-\kappa A_{1})^{-1}f\right)\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}=C\left\lVert\frac{1}{\kappa}\left(\left(I-\kappa A_{1}\right)^{-1}-I\right)f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}}{\kappa}\left(\left\lVert\left(I-\kappa A_{1}\right)^{-1}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(\Omega))}+1\right)\leq\frac{C\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}}{\kappa},

where we used

A_{1}(I-\kappa A_{1})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\kappa}((I-\kappa A_{1})^{-1}-I)
, which follows from
(I-\kappa A_{1})^{-1}(I-\kappa A_{1})=I
, as well as
Lemma
3.1
point 3.
This finishes the proof.
∎

3.4
Trace theorems

We denote the trace operator by
S
and the trace extension operator by
E
,
cf.
Section
2

and recall two results, which will be important tools in the
a priori
estimate.
First, one can relate the surface differential operators to the bulk ones.

Theorem 3.9
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded domain with
\Gamma\in C^{0,1}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
Let
f\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
.
Then the surface gradient, denoted by
\nabla_{\Gamma}
,
is just the tangential projection of the bulk gradient, that is

\nabla_{\Gamma}S(f)=S(\nabla f)-(S(\nabla f)\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\quad\text{ and }\quad\nabla_{\Gamma}S(\boldsymbol{v})=S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n}

and for the surface divergence
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot S(\boldsymbol{v})
it holds

\,\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot S(\boldsymbol{v})=S(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}.

The proof is based on
[
57
, Thm. 4.2]
and is presented in Appendix.
Secondly, we recall an integration by parts rule on the boundary.

Corollary 3.10
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded domain with
\Gamma\in C^{2,1}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
For a general (not necessarily tangential) vector field
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
and
f\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Gamma)

the following integration by parts holds

\int_{\Gamma}f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-f(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}f\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=0.

The proof can be found in
[
48
, Sec. 2.1]
and also in the Appendix,
cf.
Section
A.1
.

4
Existence of suitable weak solutions

In this section we prove Theorem
2.3
, where we proceed as follows.
We introduce a regularized system, using the resolvent of the Stokes operator and the Robin Laplacian as regularization operators.
We then show the existence of a weak solution to the regularized Nernst–Planck–Poisson subsystem via the Schaefer’s fixed point theorem.
Next, we show the local existence of a weak solution to the coupled regularized Navier–Stokes–Nernst–Planck–Poisson system
via semigroup theory and Banach’s fixed point theorem.
Deriving appropriate energy estimates we can extend this local solution to a global one
and then extract a convergent subsequence for vanishing regularization, whose limit is a weak solution to the original system (
2
).

4.1
Our regularization operators

Definition 4.1
.

Let
A_{2}
be the
L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega)
realization of the Stokes operator with
\nu=1
.
For
\kappa>0
we define

\displaystyle R_{\kappa}=\left(1+\kappa A_{2}\right)^{-1},\quad R^{1/2}_{\kappa}:=\left(1+\kappa A_{2}^{1/2}\right)^{-1},\quad\text{ and }\quad S_{\kappa}=\left(1-\kappa\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}\right)^{-1}.

Remark 4.2
.

Since
-A_{2},-A_{2}^{1/2}
and
\Delta_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}
generate
C_{0}-
semigroups in
L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega)
and
L^{2}(\Omega),
respectively,
the operators from Definition
4.1
are well-defined, linear and bounded operators.
Moreover, the operator
R^{1/2}_{\kappa}
is symmetric on
D(A_{2}^{1/2})
, which follows from a simple application of the symmetry of the root of the Stokes operator, cf. Lemma
3.3
item
4
.

4.2
Local existence of a unique weak solution to a regularized system

4.2.1
The Nernst–Planck–Poisson subsystem

We begin by fixing a velocity
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{s}(\Omega))
for some
s>3
.
Now we consider the regularized Nernst–Planck–Poisson system

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c^{\pm}+\nabla\cdot\left(c^{\pm}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\left(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm c^{\pm}\nabla\psi\right)\right)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(8a)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi)

\displaystyle=S_{\kappa}(c^{+}-c^{-})=:\varphi

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(8b)

with the initial conditions
c^{\pm}(0)=c^{\pm}_{0}\quad\text{ in }\Omega
and the boundary conditions

\left(c^{\pm}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\left(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm c^{\pm}\nabla\psi\right)\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\psi=\xi\quad\text{ on }\Gamma\times[0,T],

(9)

coupling the Nernst–Planck equation and the Poisson equation.
Our first goal is to show that this coupled system possesses a unique weak solution,
which we will prove in the next proposition using the Schaefer’s fixed point theorem.
For later reference we introduce the shorthand notation

\mathbb{W}(0,T):=L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))\cap W^{1,2}(0,T;(W^{1,2}(\Omega))^{*}).

Proposition 4.3

(Existence of weak solutions to system (
8
)–(
9
))
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a bounded domain with
\Gamma\in C^{3,1}
,

\boldsymbol{d}\in C^{2,1}(\overline{\Omega})
,

\xi\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{3,2}(\Gamma))
,

s>3
,

T\in(0,\infty)
,

\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{s}(\Omega))

and
c^{\pm}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)_{+}
.
Then there exists a unique weak solution
(c^{\pm},\psi)
such that

\displaystyle c^{\pm}\in\mathbb{W}(0,T),\quad c^{\pm}\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\psi\in C([0,T];W^{4,2}(\Omega)),

equation (
8a
) is fulfilled in the distributional sense and (
8b
) is fulfilled pointwise.

Remark 4.4
.

It is meaningful to require (
8b
) to be fulfilled pointwise since

c^{\pm}\in\mathbb{W}(0,T)\hookrightarrow C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))

and then, by Lemma
3.1
,
S_{\kappa}(c^{+}-c^{-})\in C([0,T];W^{2,2}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow C(\overline{\Omega}\times[0,T])
.

The proof of Proposition
4.3
can be conducted by following the proof of
[
27
, Lem. 4.1]
.
For completeness we
will
give a proof here but we keep
it
rather short.
We would like to point out that this proof holds without any assumptions on
\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v}
.

Proof (of Proposition
4.3
).

Let
r>3
and
X:=L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,r}(\Omega))
. For any
\widehat{\psi}\in X
there exists a unique weak solution

\widehat{c}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{W}(0,T)

of (
8a
) with
\psi
replaced by
\widehat{\psi}
, see
[
50
, Thm. 8.30 and Thm. 8.34]
.
For any such
\widehat{c}^{\pm}
there is a unique weak solution
\psi\in C([0,T];W^{4,2}(\Omega))
to (
8b
)
with right-hand side
S_{\kappa}(\widehat{c}^{+}-\widehat{c}^{-})
, see
[
31
, Thm. 2.5.1.1]
.
Thus the iterative solution operator

\mathcal{T}:X\to X,\qquad\widehat{\psi}\mapsto\psi

is well-defined.
Further, the non-negativity of the initial condition
c^{\pm}_{0}
transfers to the solution
\widehat{c}^{\pm}
, which can be seen by testing (
8a
) with

\max(0,\widehat{c}^{\pm})
using Gargliardo–Nirenberg’s and Gronwall’s inequalities in the same way as in
[
11
, p. 1395]
.
The continuity and compactness follows by testing an applying an Aubin–Lions lemma, see
[
56
, Cor. 4]
.

To show the existence of a weak solution to the coupled system (
8
) we make use of Scheafer’s fixed point theorem,
cf.
[
23
, Sec. 9, Thm. 4]
.
To that aim, we need to show that the set

M:=\left\{\psi\in X\,\lvert\,\exists\sigma\in[0,1]:\sigma\mathcal{T}(\psi)=\psi\right\}

is bounded.
So we take an arbitrary
\psi\in M
and
\sigma\in[0,1]
such that
\sigma\mathcal{T}(\psi)=\psi
.
Let
c^{\pm}
be the solution to the Nernst–Planck equation (
8a
) corresponding to
\psi
.
To get a bound of
c^{\pm}
independent of
\psi
we multiply (
8a
) by
1
and integrate to find

\int_{\Omega}c^{\pm}(t)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=\int_{\Omega}c^{\pm}_{0}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}

for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
By the non-negativity of
c^{\pm}
we find
\left\lVert c^{\pm}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\leq\left\lVert c^{\pm}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}
.
Using the elliptic
L^{1}-
regularity of the Robin Laplacian,
cf.
Lemma
3.7
,
we find that
S_{\kappa}(c^{+}-c^{-})=\varphi

is in
L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,q}(\Omega))
for all
q\in[1,3/2)
and by Corollary
3.8
and Sobolev’s embedding theorem
we find

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{t}(\Omega))}\leq C\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,q}(\Omega))}\leq\frac{C}{\kappa}\left\lVert c^{+}-c^{-}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}\leq\frac{C}{\kappa}\;\hbox to55.88pt{\vbox to17.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.70775pt\lower-8.32655pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

for all

.
Then by
[

, Thm. 2.4 and 2.6]
we find
\mathcal{T}(\psi)\in L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,t}(\Omega))

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathcal{T}(\psi)\right\rVert

where

is the trace extension operator, see Section

.
Here we used that we find an

bound for

dependent only on
\varphi
and

by testing (

) with
\mathcal{T}(\psi)
.
Thus

\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,t}(\Omega))}=\sigma\left\lVert\mathcal{T}(\psi)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,t}(\Omega))}\leq\frac{C}{\kappa}\left(\;\hbox to55.88pt{\vbox to17.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.70775pt\lower-8.32655pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt+1\right).

Since the choice of
\psi\in M
was arbitrary and for all
r\in(3,\infty)
there is
t\in[1,3)
close enough to three such that

L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,t}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,r}(\Omega)),

the boundedness of
M
in
X
follows and the Schaefer’s fixed point theorem guarantees
the existence of a fixed point of
\mathcal{T}
, which then solves our system (
8
).

Finally, the uniqueness of this fixed point, that is the uniqueness of weak solutions to the system (
8
),
follows by taking two solutions
(c^{\pm},\psi)
and
(\tilde{c}^{\pm},\tilde{\psi})
emanating from the same initial data,
subtracting (
8a
) for
c^{\pm}
and
\tilde{c}^{\pm}
and testing with the difference
(c^{\pm}-\tilde{c}^{\pm})
.
∎

To show the existence of a unique weak solution to the whole coupled system we would like to use Banach’s fixed point theorem.
In order to be able to prove that the considered mapping is a contraction,
we need an explicit formulation of some bounds for
c^{\pm}
, which we will prove next.
With explicit we mean explicit in
\boldsymbol{v}
.

Lemma 4.5

(Bounds for
c^{\pm}
)
.

Let
(c^{\pm},\psi)
be the weak solution to (
8
) with

\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{\infty}(0,T;W_{0,\sigma}^{1,2}(\Omega))
.
We then have

\displaystyle\left\lVert c^{\pm}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\left\lVert c^{\pm}\right\rVert_{L^{3}(0,T;L^{3}(\Omega))}+\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{4,2}(\Omega))}\leq C(\kappa,T),

(10)

for some function
C(\kappa,\cdot):(0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
which is strictly positive, monotonically increasing, independent of
\boldsymbol{v}

and
C(\kappa,T)<\infty
for all
T<\infty
and all
\kappa>0
.

The proof is based on elliptic regularity and a Gronwall argument and can be found in the Appendix.

4.2.2
The fully regularized system

We consider the full regularized system, similar to
[
27
]
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}+A_{2}\boldsymbol{v}+P\left((R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}\right)+R^{1/2}_{\kappa}\left(P\left((c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi\right)\right)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(11a)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c^{\pm}+\nabla\cdot\left(c^{\pm}R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\left(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm c^{\pm}\nabla\psi\right)\right)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(11b)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi)

\displaystyle=S_{\kappa}(c^{+}-c^{-})

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega\times(0,T),

(11c)

equipped with the initial conditions
\boldsymbol{v}(0)=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\text{ and }c^{\pm}(0)=c^{\pm}_{0}\text{ in }\Omega

and the boundary conditions

\boldsymbol{v}=0,\quad\left(c^{\pm}\boldsymbol{v}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\left(\nabla c^{\pm}\pm c^{\pm}\nabla\psi\right)\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0,\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\psi=\xi\quad\text{ on }\Gamma\times[0,T].

(12)

We can show the existence of a unique local weak solution to system (
11
) via the variation of constants formula
and the contraction mapping theorem.
We define

\displaystyle F_{\kappa}:D(A_{2}^{1/2})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}(\Omega),\quad

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}\mapsto P\left((R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}\right),

\displaystyle G_{\kappa}:D(A_{2}^{1/2})

\displaystyle\to L^{2}(\Omega),\quad

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}\mapsto R^{1/2}_{\kappa}\left(P\left((c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi\right)\right),

where
(c^{\pm},\psi)
in
G_{\kappa}
denotes the unique solution to the Nernst–Planck–Poisson subsystem from Proposition
4.3
.
Additionally, we define the space

X_{T}:=C\left([0,T];D(A^{1/2}_{2})\right).

Equipped with the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{X_{T}}
given by

\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}\right\rVert_{X_{T}}:=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

X_{T}
is a Banach space, see
[
20
, Lem. 7.2.1]
, where the equality follows from
[
58
, Lem. 2.2.1]
.
For
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in D(A_{2}^{1/2})
we now define

H_{\kappa}:X_{T}\to X_{T},\quad\quad H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}):=\e^{-tA_{2}}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\e^{-(t-s)A_{2}}\left(F_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(s))+G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(s))\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s,

(13)

where the integral is understood in the Bochner sense with values in
L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega)
.
The integrand of (
13
) is indeed Bochner integrable, since

t\mapsto\e^{-tA_{2}}g
is continuous from
[0,T]
to
L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega)
for all
g\in L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega)
by the strong continuity of analytic semigroups,
[
22
, Prop. 4.3]

and
t\mapsto F_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))+G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))
is continuous from
[0,T]
to
L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega)
by the boundedness of

P,R_{\kappa}
and
R^{1/2}_{\kappa}
,
cf.
[
55
, Thm. 1.4]
and Lemma
3.1
, respectively, the definition of
X_{T}
,
and the fact that by Remark
4.4
we have
(c^{\pm},\psi)\in C([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))\times C([0,T];W^{4,2}(\Omega))
.
Furthermore, we note that
H_{\kappa}
indeed maps to
X_{T}
. First, we see that for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in X_{T}
we have

H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})(t)\in D(A_{2}^{1/2})
,
since
H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})(0)=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in D(A_{2}^{1/2})
and for
t>0
we have
\e^{-tA_{2}}g\in D(A_{2})\subseteq D(A^{1/2}_{2})
for all
g\in L^{2}_{\sigma}(\Omega)
,
cf.

[
58
, Lem. 2.2.1]
.
The continuity of
t\mapsto H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))
from
[0,T]
to
W^{1,2}(\Omega)
follows from
[
40
, Prop. 4.2.1]
.

For the existence of regularized solutions we take smoother data and collect all needed assumptions in the following:

Assumption 4.6
.

We require
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}
to be a bounded smooth domain and take

{\boldsymbol{d}\in W^{4,\infty}(\Omega)}
with
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
,

{\xi\in C^{1}([0,T];W^{3,10/3}(\Gamma))}
,
\varepsilon,\lambda>0
,
{c^{\pm}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)_{+}}
, and
\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\in D(A_{2}^{1/2})
.

Lemma 4.7

(Local weak solution to system (
11
))
.

Let
T_{0}>0
and Assumption
4.6
hold, then there exists
M>\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\right\rVert_{D(A_{2}^{1/2})}
and
T^{\star}\in(0,T_{0})
such that
H_{\kappa}
is a contraction on

Z(M,T^{\star}):=\{\boldsymbol{v}\in X_{T^{\star}}|\lx@nobreakspace\boldsymbol{v}(0)=\boldsymbol{v}_{0},\;\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}\right\rVert_{X_{T^{\star}}}\leq M\}

and has a unique fixed point which solves the regularized system (
11
). Here
T^{\star}
may depend on
M
.

This proof is very similar to the proof of
[
27
, Lem. 4.2]
.

Proof.

For
T\in(0,T_{0})
the set
Z(M,T)
is a closed subset of the Banach space
X_{T}
and thus
by Banach’s fixed point theorem,
cf.
[
20
, Thm. A.2.2]
,
it is enough to show that
H_{\kappa}
is a self-map from
Z(M,T)
to
Z(M,T)
and contracting.
We first show the self-map property. We note that for
\boldsymbol{v}\in Z(M,T)
we have
H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})(0)=\boldsymbol{v}_{0}
,
thus the only thing we need to show is that
\left\lVert H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})\right\rVert_{X_{T}}\leq M
.
We start by estimating
F_{\kappa}
and
G_{\kappa}
separately. For almost all
t\in(0,T)
we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert F_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\kappa)\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C(\kappa)\left(\big\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}(t)\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C(\kappa)M^{2}

by Lemma
3.1
since
D(A_{2})\hookrightarrow W^{2,2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\Omega)
.
Additionally, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert(c^{+}(t)-c^{-}(t))\nabla\psi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\;\hbox to89.24pt{\vbox to17.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.38545pt\lower-8.32655pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-38.14018pt}{-1.95834pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -52.77 -2.71)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -56.99 0 L -56.99 11.24 L -51.08 11.24}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -56.99 0 L -56.99 -11.24 L -51.08 -11.24}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 56.99 0 L 56.99 11.24 L 51.08 11.24}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 56.99 0 L 56.99 -11.24 L 51.08 -11.24}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -56.99 9.28 L -55.02 0 L -56.99 -9.28 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 56.99 9.28 L 55.02 0 L 56.99 -9.28 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{40.21129pt}{6.3075pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 55.64 8.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{4,2}(\Omega))}\leq C(\kappa,T_{0})

by (
10
),
where the upper bound on the right is independent of
\boldsymbol{v}
and thus of
M
but depends on the boundary and initial data and follows from Lemma
4.5
.
Next, we estimate

\displaystyle\left\lVert H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\e^{-tA_{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega))}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{0}^{t}\big\lVert\e^{-(t-s)A_{2}}\big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega))}\left(\left\lVert F_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(s))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(s))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s

\displaystyle\leq C_{S}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+T(C(\kappa)M^{2}+C(\kappa,T_{0})),

where we used the boundedness of the semigroup generated by the Stokes operator, see Lemma
3.3
item
1
,
which means that there is a
C_{S}>0
such that for all
t\in(0,\infty)
we have
\left\lVert\e^{-tA_{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq C_{S}
.
Further, we have

\displaystyle\big\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(t))\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\big\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}\e^{-tA_{2}}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{0}^{t}\big\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}\e^{-(t-s)A_{2}}\big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega))}\left(\left\lVert F_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(s))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}(s))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s

\displaystyle\leq\big\lVert\e^{-tA_{2}}A_{2}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-s}}\left(C(\kappa)M^{2}+C(\kappa,T_{0})\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s

\displaystyle\leq C_{S}\big\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\sqrt{T}\left(C(\kappa)M^{2}+C(\kappa,T_{0})\right),

where we used the estimate

\left\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}\e^{-tA_{2}}\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L_{\sigma}^{2}(\Omega))}\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{t\;}}

(14)

for all
t>0
for the root of the Stokes operator, see for example
[
30
, Prop. 1.2]

and the fact that for every
g\in D(A_{2}^{1/2})
we have

A_{2}^{1/2}\e^{-tA_{2}}g=\e^{-tA_{2}}A_{2}^{1/2}g,

(15)

which follows from
[
58
, Chap. IV, below Lem. 1.5.1]
.
First choosing
M>0
large enough such that

C_{S}\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\big\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\leq\frac{M}{2}

and then choosing
T^{\star}>0
small enough so that

\Big(T^{\star}+\sqrt{T^{\star}}\,\Big)\left(C(\kappa)M^{2}+C(\kappa,T_{0})\right)\leq\frac{M}{2},

the self-map property of
H_{\kappa}
follows.
Next, we show that
H_{\kappa}
is a contraction.
For that we take
\boldsymbol{v}_{1},\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\in Z(M,T)
and by Lemma
3.1
we estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left\lVert F_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))-F(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\nabla(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))-R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\leq C(\kappa)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\big\lVert A_{2}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C(\kappa)M\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t)-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\leq C(\kappa)M\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\right\rVert_{X_{T}}.\end{split}

(16)

Let
(c^{\pm}_{1},\psi_{1})
and
(c^{\pm}_{2},\psi_{2})
be the solutions to the Nernst–Planck–Poisson system associated to
\boldsymbol{v}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{v}_{2}
respectively.
Subtracting the Nernst–Planck equations for
c^{\pm}_{1}
and
c^{\pm}_{2}
, testing with their difference, and adding the equations for the positve and negative charges,
we obtain

f\in W^{1,2}(\Omega)

) gives us

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{40.21129pt}{6.3075pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 55.64 8.73)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt\left\lVert\nabla(\psi_{1}-\psi_{2})(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\right)\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\leq C(\kappa,T_{0})\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\right\rVert_{X_{T}}\e^{C(\kappa,T_{0})T_{0}},\end{split}

(24)

where we used (
10
), (
22
) and (
23
).
Putting (
16
) and (
24
) together we get the contraction property of
H_{\kappa}
for

T^{\star}\in(0,T_{0})\text{ such that }\left(C(\kappa,T_{0})\e^{C(\kappa,T_{0})T_{0}}+C(\kappa)M\right)\left(T^{\star}+\sqrt{T^{\star}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2},

which follows from

\displaystyle\left\lVert H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1})-H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2})\right\rVert_{X_{T}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\left\lVert H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))-H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert A^{1/2}_{2}\left(H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))-H_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq C_{S}\int_{0}^{T}\left\lVert F_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))-F(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))-G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt+C\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{\sqrt{t-s}}\left(\left\lVert F_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))-F(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{1}(t))-G_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{2}(t))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\left(C(\kappa,T_{0})\e^{C(\kappa,T_{0})T_{0}}+C(\kappa)M\right)\left(T+\sqrt{T}\right)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\right\rVert_{X_{T}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{1}-\boldsymbol{v}_{2}\right\rVert_{X_{T}},

where we again used the boundedness of the semigroup generated by the Stokes operator,
the interchanging of the Stokes semigroup and the root of the Stokes operator,
cf.
(
15
),
and the estimate for the root of the Stokes operator (
14
).
∎

Proof.

We start off by testing (
11a
) with
\boldsymbol{v}
.
Since
\boldsymbol{v}\in X_{T_{\textrm{max}}}
we find by using the equation (
11a
) that

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{\infty}(0,T_{\textrm{max}},W^{-1,2}(\Omega))

and thus by the weak* density of
C^{\infty}_{c}(0,T_{\textrm{max}})\otimes D(A^{1/2}_{2})

in
L^{\infty}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};D(A^{1/2}_{2}))

[
49
, Lem. A.2.2]
,
we are allowed to test (
11a
) with
\boldsymbol{v}
it self.
By an integration by parts, see for example
[
20
, Cor. 8.1.10]
, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\bigg|_{0}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}+R_{\kappa}^{1/2}(P((c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi))\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s=0

for almost all
t\in[0,T_{\textrm{max}})
.
Since
R^{1/2}_{\kappa}
is symmetric on
D(A^{1/2}_{2})
due to Remark
4.2
, we can rewrite this as

\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\bigg|_{0}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}+(c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi\cdot R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s=0,

(27)

where we can omit the Helmholtz projection since
R_{\kappa}^{1/2}(\boldsymbol{v})
is already divergence free.
Next, we test (
11b
) with
(\ln(c^{\pm}+\delta)+2\pm\psi)
for some
\delta\in(0,1)
,
which is a well-defined test function since

by Proposition
4.3
.
By maximal

regularity for the diffusion part of the Nernst–Planck equations (

)
[

, Thm. 2.1]

and since

we find
\partial_{t}c^{\pm}\in L^{3/2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{3/2}(\Omega))

and
by Lemma
4.5
, we have

c^{\pm}\in L^{3}(0;T_{\textrm{max}};L^{3}(\Omega))

and
\psi\in L^{\infty}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{4,2}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow L^{3}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{3}(\Omega))
such that
we know that

is an admissible test function.

(28)

We note that

\displaystyle\nabla c^{\pm}=2\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta}\;\nabla\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta},\qquad\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta}\;\nabla\ln(c^{\pm}+\delta)=2\nabla\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\left((c^{\pm}+\delta)(\ln(c^{\pm}+\delta)+1)\right)=\partial_{t}c^{\pm}(\ln(c^{\pm}+\delta)+2),

which holds by the product and the chain rule for weak derivatives,
[
61
, Prob. 21.3 d)+e)]
.

(29)

\lxSVG@closescope {{

The last term on the right-hand side can be absorbed into the left-hand side and all other terms are bounded independently of
\delta
,
since
c^{\pm}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
.
Thus we have that
\nabla\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta}
is bounded in
L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T_{\textrm{max}}))
independently of
\delta
and so,
by the reflexivity of this space, we find

\nabla\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta}\rightharpoonup a
in
L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T_{\textrm{max}}))
for
\delta\searrow 0
and some

a\in L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T_{\textrm{max}}))
.
Since
\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta}\to\sqrt{c^{\pm}}
pointwise almost everywhere for
\delta\searrow 0
and

|\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta}|\leq\sqrt{c^{\pm}+1}\in L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T_{\textrm{max}}))
,
since we chose
\delta\in(0,1)
, we find by the dominated convergence theorem

a=\nabla\sqrt{c^{\pm}}
.
Now, we can pass to the limit with
\delta\searrow 0
in (
29
).
We use dominated convergence for the term including
\delta
and
\boldsymbol{v}
in the first line since
|c^{\pm}/(c^{\pm}+\delta)|\leq 1
.
We use a generalized Fatou’s lemma for functions bounded from below on finite domains,
[
19
, Chap. 5, Ex 5.4]
,
for the terms in the first line dependent on
t
.
Using
c^{\pm}_{0}\in L^{2}(\Omega)
we can apply dominated convergence to find

\lim_{\delta\searrow 0}\int_{\Omega}(c^{\pm}_{0}+\delta)(\ln(c^{\pm}_{0}+\delta)+1)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=\int_{\Omega}c^{\pm}_{0}(\ln c^{\pm}_{0}+1)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}.

Using
\ln(x)\leq x-1
and
x(\ln(x)+1)\geq-1/\e^{2}
for all
x>0
we can find a majorant by estimating,

\left|(c^{\pm}_{0}+\delta)(\ln(c^{\pm}_{0}+\delta)+1)+\frac{1}{\e^{2}}\right|\leq(c^{\pm}_{0}+\delta)^{2}+\frac{1}{\e^{2}}\leq\left(c^{\pm}_{0}\right)^{2}+2c^{\pm}_{0}+1+\frac{1}{\e^{2}}\in L^{1}(\Omega).

The integral in the last line of (
29
) vanishes for
\delta\searrow 0
, since

|\delta/\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta}|=\sqrt{\delta}\;\big|\sqrt{\delta}/\sqrt{c^{\pm}+\delta}\big|\leq\sqrt{\delta}
and thus

\displaystyle\left|\;\hbox to251.57pt{\vbox to15.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 122.54907pt\lower-7.30376pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

(30)

and since
R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v})
is divergence free the last term on the first line vanishes.
To rewrite the remaining term including the time derivative of
c^{\pm}
we differentiate (
11c
) in time and test with
\psi
.
By maximal
L^{p}-
regularity we already have
\partial_{t}c^{\pm}\in L^{3/2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{3/2}(\Omega))
.
We then find
\varphi\in W^{1,3/2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{2,3/2}(\Omega))

and
\psi\in W^{1,3/2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{4,3/2}(\Omega))

by elliptic regularity, see for example
[
31
, Thm. 2.5.1.1]

and thus we can perform the following integration by parts,

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}(c^{+}-c^{-})\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\partial_{t}\psi\cdot\nabla\psi+\kappa\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\partial_{t}\varphi\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\\
-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\psi\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\partial_{t}\psi+\kappa\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\partial_{t}\varphi\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s.

(31)

We can also differentiate the Robin boundary conditions for
\psi
and
\varphi
in time and since
\boldsymbol{d}
is independent of time we obtain

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\partial_{t}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\partial_{t}\psi=\partial_{t}\xi\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\partial_{t}\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\partial_{t}\varphi=0\quad\text{ on }\Gamma.

(32)

Testing (
11c
) with
\partial_{t}\varphi
we obtain

\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\partial_{t}\varphi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s-\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\partial_{t}\varphi\;\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\varphi|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\bigg|_{0}^{t}.

(33)

Plugging (
32
) and (
33
) back into (
31
) we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}&\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}(c^{+}-c^{-})\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\psi|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\bigg|_{0}^{t}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\varphi|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\bigg|_{0}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\kappa\partial_{t}\varphi(\xi-\tau\psi)-\psi(\partial_{t}\xi-\tau\partial_{t}\psi-\tau\kappa\partial_{t}\varphi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\\
&=\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\psi|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\varphi|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\tau}{2}\int_{\Gamma}|\psi|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\right)\bigg|_{0}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\kappa\partial_{t}\varphi\xi-\psi\partial_{t}\xi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s.\end{split}

(34)

Inserting (
34
) into (
30
) and adding (
27
) we obtain

,

.
Plugging this back into (

) we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hskip-10.00002pt\left(\int_{\Omega}\frac{1}{2}|\boldsymbol{v}|^{2}+\;\hbox to66.22pt{\vbox to15.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 29.87842pt\lower-7.30376pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt+\frac{1}{2}|\nabla\psi|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}+\frac{\kappa}{2}|\varphi|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\frac{\tau}{2}\int_{\Gamma}|\psi|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\right)\bigg|_{0}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\\
&\leq C\left\lVert\xi(0)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}+C\left\lVert\xi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\tau}{2}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\frac{1}{2\tau}\left\lVert\partial_{t}\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+C\left\lVert\partial_{t}\xi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\\
&\leq C\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{C([0,T_{\textrm{max}}];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{W^{1,1}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{\infty}(\Omega))}\right)+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\tau}{2}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\\
&\leq C\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{W^{1,1}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{\infty}(\Omega))}\right)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)(s)+\frac{2|\Omega|}{\e^{2}}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s,\end{split}

(36)

with
C>0
independent of
\kappa,\boldsymbol{d}
and
\xi
,
where we added the constant
\frac{2|\Omega|}{\e^{2}}
to make the energy non-negative (
c^{\pm}(\ln c^{\pm}+1)+1/\e^{2}\geq 0
),
so that we can estimate
\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)+2|\Omega|/\mathrm{\e}^{2}
,
and used the embedding

W^{1,1}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{\infty}(\Gamma))\hookrightarrow C([0,T_{\textrm{max}}];L^{\infty}(\Gamma)),

cf.
[
50
, Lem. 7.1]
.
Now we can apply Gronwall’s inequality to infer

\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\\
\leq\e^{t}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0},c^{\pm}_{0},\psi_{0})+C\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{W^{1,1}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{\infty}(\Omega))}+T_{\textrm{max}}\right)\right).

By the continuity of
(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)
,
cf.
Remark
4.4
and Lemma
4.7

this inequality holds for all
t\in[0,T_{\textrm{max}})
,
which finishes our proof.
∎

From Proposition
4.8
we can derive a second energy estimate.
This is achieved through integration by parts, with the primary technical challenge being the control of second-order derivatives of the electric potential on the boundary.
We address this challenge using the surface gradient, surface divergence, and integration by parts on the boundary.
The necessary results on surface differential operators are provided in the Appendix,
cf.
Section
A.1
, for the reader’s convenience.
The integration by parts on the boundary then enables us to plug in the Robin boundary condition for the electric potential,
thereby reducing the order of derivatives of the electric potential on the boundary to a controllable level.
A crucial assumption in this step is the tangentiality of the director field
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
, ensuring that

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\varepsilon(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})=\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}.

This gives us the necessary flexibility for incorporating the Robin boundary condition.

Proposition 4.9

(Energy inequality II for (
11
))
.

Let Assumption
4.6
hold and let
(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)
be the weak solution to (
11
) on the maximal time interval
[0,T_{\textrm{max}})

for some
\kappa>0

given by Lemma
4.7
.
Then for
\kappa>0
small enough
(that is
\kappa C(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)})\leq 1/32
for some constant
C>0
),
there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Omega}\;\hbox to124.55pt{\vbox to17.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 59.0422pt\lower-7.92043pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

(37)

Proof.

This follows from extending the first energy inequality from Proposition
4.8
.
Extending the squared sum of the dissipation
\mathcal{W}
, the first energy inequality (
26
) implies

\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Omega}\;\hbox to129.55pt{\vbox to17.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.5422pt\lower-7.92043pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{60.36804pt}{5.90137pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 83.53 8.17)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt+2\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\left(\nabla c^{+}-\nabla c^{-}\right)\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\\
\leq\e^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0},c^{\pm}_{0},\psi_{0})+C\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{W^{1,1}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}+T_{\textrm{max}}\right)\right),

(38)

for some
C>0
independent of
\kappa
.
Using the elliptic
L^{2}-
estimate for the Robin Laplacian,
cf.
Lemma
3.5
and Lemma
3.1
,
we can estimate

\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\varphi_{0}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\frac{\kappa}{2}\;\hbox to55.88pt{\vbox to18.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.70775pt\lower-8.41544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-21.46248pt}{-2.04723pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -29.7 -2.83)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
