{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -33.91 0 L -33.91 11.37 L -28.01 11.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -33.91 0 L -33.91 -11.37 L -28.01 -11.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 33.91 0 L 33.91 11.37 L 28.01 11.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 33.91 0 L 33.91 -11.37 L 28.01 -11.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -33.91 9.4 L -31.94 0 L -33.91 -9.4 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 33.91 9.4 L 31.94 0 L 33.91 -9.4 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{23.53358pt}{6.39638pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 32.56 8.85)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt

and thus
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{reg}}(\boldsymbol{v}_{0},c^{\pm}_{0},\psi_{0})\leq C
.
The first two terms on the left-hand side of (
38
) already have a the right sign so we turn directly to the third term
and integrate it by parts to see that in the end it also gives a “good” term at least in the terms quadratic in the highest order derivatives
of
\psi
:

\int_{\Omega}2\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\left(\nabla c^{+}-\nabla c^{-}\right)\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
=-\int_{\Omega}2(c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}2(c^{+}-c^{-})(\xi-\tau\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,

(39)

where we used the Robin boundary condition for the electric potential and the fact that
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
and thus

\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}.

Using the regularized Poisson equation
cf.
(
11c
), we can rewrite the volume integral of (
39
) as

-\int_{\Omega}2(c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=-\int_{\Omega}2\varphi\;\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
-\kappa\int_{\Omega}2\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\kappa\int_{\Gamma}2\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\;\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

(40)

The first term on the right-hand side turns out to give us the term with second order derivatives of
\psi
with a “good” sign
needed for (
37
).
To see that, we again use the regularized Poisson equation (
11c
),

-\int_{\Omega}2\varphi\;\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=2\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\;\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
=2\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)+\varepsilon\lambda|\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})|^{2}+(\varepsilon+\lambda)(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}.

(41)

The first and second term already have a good sign but to get a term with a good sign in the full second order derivative of
\psi

we integrate the first term by parts two times
and to see that the third term also has a positive sign in the term quadratic in the second order derivative of
\psi

we also integrate it by parts two times.
Starting with the first term on the right-hand side of (
41
), we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}&=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Omega}(\nabla^{2}\psi)^{T}:\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi)^{T}\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{2}\psi|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi)\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)(\xi-\tau\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(42)

The first term is exactly the one we aimed for so we move to the boundary terms.
To be able to take the full gradient of the outer normal field
\boldsymbol{n}
we introduce the trace extension operator
E
and the trace operator
S
,
this can be seen in the following calculation. For the sake of readability we will again omit
E
and
S
in all other calculations.
It should be clear from the context if one needs to consider a function on the boundary or in the bulk.
Using the characterization of the surface gradient
\nabla_{\Gamma}
,

cf.
Theorem
3.9
,
we can rewrite the first boundary integral on the right-hand side of (
42
) as

\displaystyle\begin{split}-&\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi)\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}S\left([\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot E(\boldsymbol{n}))-\nabla E(\boldsymbol{n})^{T}\nabla\psi]\cdot\nabla\psi\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=-\int_{\Gamma}\left[\nabla_{\Gamma}(S(\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{n})+(S(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot E(\boldsymbol{n})))\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}-S(\nabla E(\boldsymbol{n})^{T}\nabla\psi)\right]\cdot S(\nabla\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}S(\nabla E(\boldsymbol{n})^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\psi)-\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau S(\psi))\cdot S(\nabla\psi)-(S(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot E(\boldsymbol{n})))\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\xi-\tau S(\psi))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}S(\nabla E(\boldsymbol{n})^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\psi)-\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau S(\psi))\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}S(\psi)+(S(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot E(\boldsymbol{n})))\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\xi-\tau S(\psi))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\tau|\nabla_{\Gamma}S(\psi)|^{2}-\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}S(\psi)-(S(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot E(\boldsymbol{n})))\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\xi-\tau S(\psi))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}S(\nabla E(\boldsymbol{n})^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,\end{split}

(43)

where we used the integration by parts rule from Corollary
3.10
two times to plug in the Robin boundary condition
and since
\nabla\psi
is not necessarily tangential on the boundary a curvature term appears,
but as we integrated by parts two times it vanishes again.
The first term has a good sign, the second and last term can be estimated due to the assumed regularity of
\xi
and
\boldsymbol{n}

and the third term cancels with part of the second boundary integral in (
42
).
Which follows from rewriting the second boundary integral on the right-hand side of (
42
)
with the help of the surface divergence Theorem
3.9
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)(\xi-\tau\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma&=\int_{\Gamma}[\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla\psi)+\nabla(\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}](\xi-\tau\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}-\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)+(\xi-\tau\psi)(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\xi-\tau\psi)-\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\xi-\tau\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\tau|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi|^{2}-\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi+(\xi-\tau\psi)^{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\xi-\tau\psi)-\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\xi-\tau\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(44)

The first term again has a good sign, the second, third and last can be absorbed
and the fourth cancels with the third term on the right-hand side of (
43
).
We now turn to the third term on the right-hand side of (
41
) and proceed similarly.
Integrating this term by parts two times, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}&\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla\psi\cdot(\nabla\cdot\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})^{T})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\;(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\nabla^{2}\psi:\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(45)

All terms quadratic in the second derivative of
\psi
in the matrix-scalar product have a good sign
and the boundary integrals can partly be absorbed and partly cancel each other out.
The following calculation shows that the terms quadratic in the second derivative of
\psi
in the matrix-scalar product have a good sign.
Using the symmetry of
\nabla^{2}\psi
we have
\nabla^{2}\psi:\boldsymbol{A}^{T}=\nabla^{2}\psi:\boldsymbol{A}
for any matrix
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
and

\nabla\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}\right)=(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)+\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}).

Thus we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla^{2}\psi:\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})^{T}&=\nabla^{2}\psi:\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})\\
&=\nabla^{2}\psi:\left(\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})+(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\right)\\
&=|\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}+\nabla^{2}\psi:\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\right).\end{split}

(46)

The first term on the right-hand side of (
46
) has a good sign and the second term can be handled by Young’s inequality.
We collect the lower order terms (
\mathrm{l.o.t.}
) in

\displaystyle\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega}=(\varepsilon+\lambda)\nabla^{2}\psi:\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\right).

(47)

We now turn to the boundary integrals on the right-hand side of (
45
).
The first boundary term on the right-hand side of (
45
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\begin{split}-&\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}\left[\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\right]\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=-\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\nabla\psi+(\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\nabla\psi-(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\xi-\tau\psi)-(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,\end{split}

(48)

where we used the fact that
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
.
For the second boundary term on the right-hand side of (
45
) we note

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)(\nabla&\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=\int_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\left[\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})+\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\right]\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}-\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})+(\xi-\tau\psi)\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt-\int_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\tau|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}-(\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-(\xi-\tau\psi)(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,\end{split}

(49)

where we again used the integration by parts rule on the boundary from Corollary
3.10
,
since the vector field
\boldsymbol{d}
is indeed tangential there is no curvature term.
The first term has a good sign, the second and last term can be absorbed and the third term cancels with
the first term on the right-hand side of (
48
).
We collect the lower order boundary terms of (
43
), (
44
), (
48
) and (
49
) in

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Gamma}&:=-2\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi+\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\psi+(\xi-\tau\psi)^{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\xi-\tau\psi)\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+(\varepsilon+\lambda)\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi-(\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})-(\xi-\tau\psi)(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\right).\end{split}

(50)

Now, we turn to the volume integral of (
40
) with prefactor
\kappa
to see that this gives a term with good sign for the
third order derivatives of
\psi
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}&=\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi))\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\left(\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&=\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(\Delta\psi)|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}+\varepsilon\lambda|\nabla(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Omega}(\varepsilon+\lambda)\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}.\end{split}

(51)

To see that the third term also gives a good sign for the critical order term
we proceed as above, integrating by parts two times and estimating the occurring boundary term.
This calculation is quite lengthy and uses the same technique as above thus we perform it in the Appendix
for thoroughness and for the interested reader.
By Lemma
A.32
from the Appendix we have

\displaystyle(\varepsilon+\lambda)\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=(\varepsilon+\lambda)\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}+\varepsilon|(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\mathrm{l.o.t}_{\kappa}.

Finally, we consider the boundary integral of (
40
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\kappa\int_{\Gamma}2\nabla\cdot&\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\;\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=-2\kappa\tau\int_{\Gamma}\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\varphi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=2\kappa\tau\int_{\Gamma}\nabla\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\right)\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=2\kappa\tau\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\psi|^{2}+\varepsilon\lambda|\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})|^{2}+(\varepsilon+\lambda)\Delta\psi\cdot\left(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(52)

Collecting the transformations from (
40
)–(
52
)
we can rewrite (
39
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}2\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})&\left(\nabla c^{+}-\nabla c^{-}\right)\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=2\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{2}\psi|^{2}+\varepsilon\lambda|\nabla\cdot((\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})|^{2}+(\varepsilon+\lambda)|\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&+2\kappa\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(\Delta\psi)|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}^{2}+\varepsilon\lambda|\nabla(\nabla\cdot((\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}))|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&+2(\varepsilon+\lambda)\kappa\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}+\varepsilon|(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&+2\tau\int_{\Gamma}2|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi|^{2}+(\varepsilon+\lambda)|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+2\kappa\tau\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\psi|^{2}+\varepsilon\lambda|\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&+2\int_{\Gamma}(c^{+}-c^{-})(\xi-\tau\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+2\kappa\tau\int_{\Gamma}(\varepsilon+\lambda)\Delta\psi\;\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&+2\left(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Gamma}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+\kappa\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}\right).\end{split}

Inserting this back into (
38
) we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\hskip-30.00005pt\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Omega}\;\hbox to129.55pt{\vbox to17.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 61.5422pt\lower-7.92043pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\vskip-5.69046pt

&+2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Omega}(\varepsilon+\lambda)|\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}+\kappa|\nabla(\Delta\psi)|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}^{2}+\kappa\varepsilon\lambda|\nabla(\nabla\cdot((\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}))|_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&+2\kappa(\varepsilon+\lambda)\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}+\varepsilon|(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\\
&+2\tau\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}2|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi|^{2}+(\varepsilon+\lambda)|\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\\
&+2\kappa\tau\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}|\Delta\psi|^{2}+\varepsilon\lambda|\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\\
&\hskip-10.00002pt\leq C\e^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{W^{1,1}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}+T_{\textrm{max}}+1\right)\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\underbrace{-2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}(c^{+}-c^{-})(\xi-\tau\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t}_{=:\hypertarget{firstest}{\mathrm{\,I\,}}}\underbrace{-2\kappa\tau\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}(\varepsilon+\lambda)\Delta\psi\;\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t}_{=:\hypertarget{fsecondest}{\mathrm{\,II}}}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt-\underbrace{2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Gamma}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+\kappa\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t}_{=:\hypertarget{thirdest}{\mathrm{\,III}}}.\end{split}

(53)

Now, we can start to estimate the right-hand side.
For the boundary integral in
I
we use the trace estimate
[
17
, Prop. 8.2]
,
which gives us that for all
p\in[1,3)
there exists
C>0
such that for all
\delta>0
and all
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Gamma)}\leq\delta\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+C\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

(54)

holds, for
q\in\left[1,2p/(3-p)\right]
.
Choosing

\delta=(C(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}+\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{1,2}(\Omega))})+1)^{-1/2}

we obtain

\displaystyle|\hyperlink{firstest}{\mathrm{I}}|

Next, we turn to the boundary integral in
II
, where we again use the trace estimate

[
17
, Prop. 8.2]
,
cf.
(
54
) with

u=\Delta\psi
and
\delta=\left(\frac{\varepsilon\lambda}{(\varepsilon+\lambda)^{2}\tau}\right)^{1/2}
.
We find

\displaystyle|\hyperlink{secondest}{\mathrm{II}}|

\displaystyle{\leq}\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\kappa\frac{(\varepsilon+\lambda)^{2}\tau}{2\varepsilon\lambda}\left\lVert\Delta\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+2\kappa\tau\varepsilon\lambda\left\lVert\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\kappa C(\varepsilon,\lambda,\tau)\left\lVert\Delta\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+2\kappa\tau\varepsilon\lambda\left\lVert\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d})\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t.

The first term can be absorbed into the term

\kappa\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

on the left-hand side of (
53
),
the second term can be absorbed into the good term of
\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}
on the left-hand side of (
53
)
(without prefactor
\kappa
) using the smallness assumption of
\kappa

and the last term can also be absorbed into the according term on the left-hand side of (
53
).
Using Lemma
A.34
from the Appendix, we find

|\hyperlink{thirdest}{\mathrm{III}}|\leq C+\frac{\tau}{2}\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\tau\frac{\varepsilon+\lambda}{2}\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}\\
+\frac{7}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))},

Putting the estimates for
\hyperlink{firstest}{\mathrm{I}}-\hyperlink{thirdhest}{\mathrm{III}}
together,
we can rewrite (
53
) as

4.4
Limit passage

Lemma 4.10
.

Under the Assumption
4.6
,
\kappa>0
as in Lemma
4.7
, and all
T\in(0,\infty)
there exists a unique weak solution to the regularized system (
11
).

The proof is conducted analogously to the proof of
[
27
, Lem. 4.4]
.

Proof.

Let
\bar{T}>0
be such that we have a weak solution
\boldsymbol{v}\in C([0,\bar{T}];D(A_{2}^{1/2}))
to (
11a
)
given by the fixed point of Lemma
4.7
.
By
[
40
, Prop. 7.1.8]
there exists a maximal interval of existence
[0,T_{\max})
for some
T_{\max}>\bar{T}
, such that
there is a solution
\boldsymbol{v}
on
[0,T]
for all
T<T_{\textrm{max}}
and for
T_{\max}<\infty
we have

\lim_{t\nearrow T_{\max}}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{D(A_{2}^{1/2})}\nearrow\infty.

By the two energy inequalities from Propositions
4.8
and
4.9
,
we can infer the boundedness
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{D(A_{2}^{1/2})}\leq C(T_{\textrm{max}})
with
C(T_{\textrm{max}})<\infty
for
T_{\textrm{max}}<\infty
and all
t\in[0,T_{\max})
by testing the Navier–Stokes equation (
11a
) with
A_{2}(\boldsymbol{v})
and thus
T_{\max}=\infty
follows.
We now derive this bound of
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}(t)\right\rVert_{D(A_{2}^{1/2})}
.
By the energy inequalities (
26
) and (
37
) there exists a constant
C>0

depending on initial values, the external field
\xi
and the regularization coefficient
\kappa
, such that

1

\boldsymbol{v}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T_{\max};L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T_{\max};D(A_{2}^{1/2}))
by
C\e^{CT_{\textrm{max}}}
.

2

\sqrt{c^{\pm}}
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T_{\max};L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T_{\max};W^{1,2}(\Omega))
by
C\e^{CT_{\textrm{max}}}
.
This implies that
c^{\pm}
is bounded in
L^{2}(0,T_{\max};L^{3/2}(\Omega))
by
C\e^{CT_{\textrm{max}}}
.

3

By elliptic
L^{1}-
regularity we have that
\psi
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T_{\max};W^{3,q}(\Omega))
for all
q\in[1,3/2)

and thus
\nabla\psi
is bounded in
L^{\infty}(0,T_{\max};L^{q}(\Omega))
for all
q\in[1,\infty)
by
C\e^{CT_{\textrm{max}}}
.

Using these bounds and the
L^{p}-
realization of the fractional power Stokes operator,
cf.
Definition
3.2
,
Lemma
3.3
, and Lemma
3.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(P((c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(P((c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi))\right\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,6/5}(\Omega))}

\displaystyle\leq C(\kappa)\left\lVert(c^{+}-c^{-})\nabla\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{6/5}(\Omega))}

\displaystyle\leq C(\kappa)\;\hbox to92.64pt{\vbox to18.5pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 43.08688pt\lower-8.59044pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

Proposition 4.11
.

Let Assumption
2.1
hold and let
\{\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\}\subseteq W^{4,\infty}(\Omega)
and
\{\kappa\}
be sequences with
\kappa>0
and
\kappa\searrow 0
such that
\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
,
\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\to\boldsymbol{d}
pointwise almost everywhere and
strongly in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)

and
\nabla\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup^{*}\nabla\boldsymbol{d}
in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
and

C\kappa\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)\leq 1/32.

(55)

Additionally, take
\{c^{\pm}_{0\kappa}\}\subseteq L^{2}(\Omega)_{+}\cap W^{1,2}(\Omega)

and
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{0\kappa}\}\subseteq D(A_{2})
to be such that

\displaystyle c^{\pm}_{0\kappa}\to c^{\pm}_{0}\quad\text{ in }L^{2}(\Omega)_{+}\quad\text{ and }\quad\boldsymbol{v}_{0\kappa}\to\boldsymbol{v}_{0}\quad\text{ in }L^{2}_{\sigma}(\Omega),

and
\{\xi_{\kappa}\}\subseteq\,C^{\infty}([0,T])\otimes C^{\infty}(\Gamma)
such that
\,\xi_{\kappa}\to\xi
in
W^{1,2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Gamma))
.
Then there exists a subsequence of global weak solutions to (
11
), from Lemma
4.10
,
which we call
(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa},c^{\pm}_{\kappa},\psi_{\kappa})
such that

\displaystyle\psi_{0\kappa}

\displaystyle\to\psi_{0}

\displaystyle\text{ in }W^{1,2}(\Omega),

(56a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}_{\sigma}(\Omega)),

(56b)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\to\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle\text{ in }C_{w}([0,T];L^{2}_{\sigma}(\Omega)),

(56c)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\to\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}_{\sigma}(\Omega)),

(56d)

\displaystyle\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\sqrt{c^{\pm}}

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega)),

(56e)

\displaystyle c^{\pm}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\to c^{\pm}

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{5/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))\cap L^{1}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))\text{ for all }p\in[1,3),

(56f)

\displaystyle\nabla c^{\pm}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\nabla c^{\pm}

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T)),

(56g)

\displaystyle c^{\pm}_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup c^{\pm}

\displaystyle\text{ in }W^{1,10/9}(0,T;W^{-1,10/9}(\Omega)),

(56h)

\displaystyle c^{\pm}_{\kappa}(t)

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup c^{\pm}(t)

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{1}(\Omega)\text{ for \lx@text@underline{all} }t\in[0,T],

(56i)

\displaystyle\psi_{\kappa}(t)

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\psi(t)

\displaystyle\text{ in }W^{1,2}(\Omega)\text{ for \lx@text@underline{all} }t\in[0,T],

(56j)

\displaystyle\psi_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\psi

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Omega)),

(56k)

\displaystyle\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\nabla\psi_{\kappa}

\displaystyle\rightharpoonup\sqrt{c^{\pm}}\nabla\psi

\displaystyle\text{ in }L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)),

(56l)

for
\kappa\searrow 0
,
whose limit fulfills the weak formulation (
3
)–(
5
) for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Remark 4.12

(Existence of approximate sequences for the director field and the external electric potential)
.

The existence of a sequence
\{\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\}
fulfilling the properties assumed in Proposition
4.11
can be proven via
a standard mollification,
[
23
, Sec. C.4]
.
In
[
23
, Sec. 5.3.3]
the density of
C^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
in
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
is proven.
A straight forward calculation proves that the approximating sequence given in the proof there also fulfills the convergence
\nabla\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup^{*}\nabla\boldsymbol{d}
in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
. The standard mollification can be altered, to ensure that all elements of the approximating sequence already
fulfill the boundary condition, that is
\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
for all
\kappa
.
The existence of a sequence
\{\xi_{\kappa}\}
with the properties from the lemma follows from the density
of
C^{\infty}(\Gamma)
in
W^{2,2}(\Gamma)
, cf.
[
38
, Sec. 4.3]
,
then
[
52
, Thm. 5.12]
gives the density of
C^{\infty}([0,T])\otimes C^{\infty}(\Gamma)
in
W^{1,2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Gamma))
.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}
be the solution to

\displaystyle\tilde{\varphi}-\kappa\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\tilde{\varphi})

\displaystyle=c^{+}_{0\kappa}-c^{-}_{0\kappa}

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega,

(57a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\tilde{\varphi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\tilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ on }\Gamma.

(57b)

Then by Lemma
3.1
, point
1
, we have
\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}\to c^{+}_{0}-c^{-}_{0}
as
\kappa\to 0
.
Note, that here we took
A
from Lemma
3.1
to be
\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\cdot)
,
and thus independent of
\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}
. Thus the lemma is indeed applicable.
To find the convergence of
\varphi_{0\kappa}
, the solution to

\displaystyle\varphi-\kappa\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa})\nabla\varphi)

\displaystyle=c^{+}_{0\kappa}-c^{-}_{0\kappa}

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega,

(58a)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa})\nabla\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\varphi

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ on }\Gamma.

(58b)

to
c^{+}_{0}-c^{-}_{0}
, we show that
\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}
goes to zero.
With that, the convergence
\varphi_{0\kappa}\to c^{+}_{0}-c^{-}_{0}
follows by the triangle inequality.
We now show
\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}\to 0
, by subtracting (
57a
),
the equation for
\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}
,
from the equation (
58a
) for
\varphi_{0\kappa}
, and testing with the difference

\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}
, which yields

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|\varphi_{0\kappa}

\displaystyle-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\kappa\int_{\Omega}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa})\nabla\varphi_{0\kappa}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}\right)\cdot\nabla(\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\tau\int_{\Gamma}|\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}|\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\tau\int_{\Gamma}|\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+\kappa\int_{\Omega}|\nabla(\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa})|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa})}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\kappa\int_{\Omega}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa})-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\right)\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}\cdot\nabla(\varphi_{0\kappa}-\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=0.

Using Young’s inequality on the last term and the estimate

\kappa\left\lVert\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{0\kappa}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\;\hbox to58.3pt{\vbox to18.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 25.91669pt\lower-8.41544pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

(60)

{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.58 0 L 35.58 11.37 L 29.68 11.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.58 0 L 35.58 -11.37 L 29.68 -11.37}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{

{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -35.58 9.4 L -33.62 0 L -35.58 -9.4 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 35.58 9.4 L 33.62 0 L 35.58 -9.4 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{24.74252pt}{6.39638pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 34.24 8.85)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt+(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)})\left\lVert\psi_{0\kappa}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}\right)\leq C,

(62)

with
C>0
independent of
\kappa
, since convergent sequences are bounded,
where we used the elliptic estimate for the Robin Laplacian in
L^{2}(\Omega)
,

cf.
Lemma
3.5
and Lemma
3.1
,
and the fact that

\{\xi_{\kappa}\}\text{ is bounded in }W^{1,2}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Gamma))

(63)

independently from
\kappa
.
This boundedness follows from the fact that
\{\xi_{\kappa}\}
is bounded in
W^{1,2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Gamma))
, since it is convergent in that space,
and from the embedding
W^{2,2}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\Gamma)
,
cf.
[
15
, Thm. 3.81]
.
Thus we have the boundedness of
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}\}
in

\mathbb{V}:=L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}_{\sigma}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T)),

(64)

where the embedding is a simple consequence of Hölder’s and Sobolev’s inequalities.
By the boundedness of
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}\}
in the space
\mathbb{V}
the weak convergence (
56b
) follows directly.
We use an Aubin–Lions lemma to deduce the strong
L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))-
convergence of
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}\}
.
To get an estimate for the time derivative of
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}\}
we use the equation
(
11a
) and write

\partial_{t}\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}=-A_{2}\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}-P((R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}))-P(R^{1/2}_{\kappa}((c^{+}_{\kappa}-c^{-}_{\kappa})\nabla\psi_{\kappa})).

(65)

The first term on the right-hand side is bounded in
L^{2}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega))
and the second term is bounded in
L^{5/4}(0,T;L^{5/4}(\Omega))
, which we will prove below.
Over all the right-hand side is bounded in

L^{5/4}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega))
. Using the compact Aubin–Lions embedding

L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))\cap W^{1,5/4}(0,T;W^{-1,2}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))

we obtain the strong convergence (
56d
).
The boundedness of
\{\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}\}
in
L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
from the energy inequality (
26
) together with
[
18
, Prop. 4.9]
, gives (
56c
), at least along a subsequence.
We now show that the second term on the right-hand side of (
65
) is indeed bounded in
L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T))
independently of
\kappa
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert P((R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}))\right\rVert_{L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T))}

\displaystyle{\leq}\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\right\rVert_{L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}

\displaystyle{\leq}C\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\underset{\eqref{eq:L103_bound_v}}{\leq}C.

Next, we estimate the third term on the right-hand side of (
65
)

\displaystyle\left\lVert R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(P((c^{+}_{\kappa}-c^{-}_{\kappa})\nabla\psi_{\kappa}))\right\rVert_{L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T))}

\displaystyle{\leq}C\left\lVert(c^{+}_{\kappa}-c^{-}_{\kappa})\nabla\psi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T))}

\displaystyle{\leq}C\;\hbox to179.81pt{\vbox to17.81pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 86.67204pt\lower-8.246pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-83.42677pt}{-2.0389pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -115.44 -2.82)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -119.65 0 L -119.65 11.13 L -113.75 11.13}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -119.65 0 L -119.65 -11.13 L -113.75 -11.13}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 119.65 0 L 119.65 11.13 L 113.75 11.13}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{{}{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 119.65 0 L 119.65 -11.13 L 113.75 -11.13}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{
{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -119.65 9.16 L -117.68 0 L -119.65 -9.16 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{}{{}}{}{{}{}{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}{
{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 119.65 9.16 L 117.68 0 L 119.65 -9.16 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{85.49788pt}{6.22694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 118.3 8.62)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\hskip-1.42271pt,

by the energy estimates (
26
) and (
37
) the right-hand side is bounded, since by these estimates we have that
\big\{\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\,}\,\big\}
is bounded in

L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow L^{{10}/{3}}(\Omega\times(0,T)).

(66)

We finished proving that the third term on the right-hand side of (
65
) is bounded in
L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T))

and thus we have proven the strong convergence in
(
56d
).
With this strong convergence we can deduce
R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\to\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}
and
R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\to\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}
in

L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))
by Lemma
3.1
.

From the boundedness of
\big\{\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\;\big\}
in (
66
) and the equation
\nabla c^{\pm}_{\kappa}=2\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\nabla\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}
, we infer the estimate,

\displaystyle\|\nabla c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\|_{L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\leq 2\big\|\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\big\|_{L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\big\|\nabla\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\big\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\leq C\,.

(67)

Using the equations (
11b
), we may estimate the time derivative
\partial_{t}c^{\pm}_{\kappa}
via

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\|_{L^{10/9}(0,T;W^{-1,10/9}(\Omega))}\leq{}

\displaystyle\big\|-R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})c^{\pm}_{\kappa}+\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa})(\nabla c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\pm c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\nabla\psi_{\kappa})\big\|_{L^{10/9}(\Omega\times(0,T))}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\big\|R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\big\|_{L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\big\|\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\big\|_{L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}^{2}

\displaystyle+C\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\|\nabla c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\|_{L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T))}

\displaystyle+C\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa})\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\big\lVert\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\big\rVert_{L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\big\lVert\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\nabla\psi_{\kappa}\big\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\,.

Hence, we infer that
\{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\}
is bounded in

W^{1,10/9}(0,T;W^{-1,10/9}(\Omega))\cap L^{5/4}(0,T;W^{1,5/4}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow L^{5/4}(0,T;L^{5/4}(\Omega))

(68)

due to the Aubin–Lions lemma. From the
a priori
estimate providing the boundedness of
\{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\}
in

L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))\cap L^{1}(0,T;L^{3}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow L^{5/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))\,,

we infer
c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\to c^{\pm}
in
L^{p}(\Omega\times(0,T))
for
p<5/3
as well as
c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\to c^{\pm}
in
L^{1}(0,T;L^{r}(\Omega))
for
r<3
. This strong convergence and Lebesgue’s theorem of dominated convergence provides that
\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\to\sqrt{c^{\pm}}
in
L^{q}(\Omega\times(0,T))
for
q<10/3
.
The bound of the time derivative of
\{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\}
additionally gives us the convergence (
56h
).
The embedding
W^{1,10/9}(0,T;W^{-1,10/9}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow C([0,T];W^{-1,10/9}(\Omega))
even implies the pointwise convergence

c^{\pm}_{\kappa}(t)\rightharpoonup c^{\pm}(t)\quad\text{ in }W^{-1,10/9}(\Omega)\text{ for all }t\in[0,T]\,.

(69)

By the regularized energy (
26
), which holds for all
t\in[0,T]
, we obtain for all
t\in[0,T]
uniform (in
\kappa
) boundedness of
c^{\pm}_{\kappa}(t)
and
c^{\pm}_{\kappa}(t)\ln c^{\pm}_{\kappa}(t)
in
L^{1}(\Omega)

and by the de la Vallée–Poussin theorem,
[
14
, Thm. 2]
, we can extract a weakly convergent
sequence such that
c^{\pm}_{\kappa}(t)\rightharpoonup a(t)
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
.
With the help of the convergence (
69
) we can identify

a(t)=c(t)
and thus we obtain (
56i
).
By the regularized energy inequality we also obtain
\psi_{\kappa}(t)\rightharpoonup\chi(t)
in
W^{1,2}(\Omega)
for all
t\in[0,T]

and by the convergence (
56i
) we can identify

\chi(t)=\psi(t)
through the equation and thus we obtain (
56j
).

Finally, we turn our attention to
\{\psi_{\kappa}\}
.
By the boundedness of
\big\{\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\nabla\psi_{\kappa}\big\}
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
,
by the second energy inequality (
37
), we find
\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\nabla\psi_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup\sqrt{c^{\pm}}\nabla\psi

in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))
which gives (
56l
).
From the second energy inequality (
37
) we also find
\psi_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup\psi
in
L^{2}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Omega))
.

With the continuity properties of
R_{\kappa}
and
R^{1/2}_{\kappa}
,
cf.
Lemma
3.1
,
the convergences (
56
) are
enough to pass to the limit in the weak formulation of (
11
) and thus the limit is a weak solution to (
2
).
We only show the proof of the convergence of the nonlinear terms, as the convergence of the linear terms is
straight forward.
By the strong convergence of
R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})
in
L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))
and
the weak convergence of
\nabla\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}
in
L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))
we get the weak convergence of

(R_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\cdot\nabla)\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa}
in
L^{1}(\Omega\times(0,T))
.
By the strong convergence
\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\to\boldsymbol{d}
in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)

together with the weak convergence of
\nabla c^{\pm}_{\kappa}
,
cf.
(
56g
),
we find

\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\nabla c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\,\nabla c^{\pm}\quad\quad\text{ in }L^{5/4}(0,T;L^{5/4}(\Omega))\,.

From the estimate
\|c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\nabla\psi_{\kappa}\|_{L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\leq\|\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\|_{L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\|\sqrt{c^{\pm}_{\kappa}}\nabla\psi_{\kappa}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega\times(0,T))}
, we infer weak convergence and can immediately identify the weak limit due to the strong convergence (
56f
) such that

c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\nabla\psi_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup c^{\pm}\nabla\psi\quad\quad\text{ in }L^{5/4}(0,T;L^{5/4}(\Omega))\,.

Again using that the product of a weakly and a strongly convergent sequence is again weakly convergent, we obtain

c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\nabla\psi_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup c^{\pm}\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\,\nabla\psi\quad\quad\text{ in }L^{5/4}(\Omega\times(0,T)).

The bound
\|c^{\pm}_{\kappa}R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\|_{L^{10/9}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\leq\|c^{\pm}_{\kappa}\|_{L^{5/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\|R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\|_{L^{10/3}(\Omega\times(0,T))}\leq C
, the
continuity of
R^{1/2}_{\kappa}
together with the convergences (
56c
)
and (
56f
),
also implies

c^{\pm}_{\kappa}R^{1/2}_{\kappa}(\boldsymbol{v}_{\kappa})\rightharpoonup c^{\pm}\boldsymbol{v}\quad\quad\text{ in }L^{10/9}(\Omega\times(0,T))\,.

Now, we have shown that we can pass to the limit in the weak formulation (
4
) for the charged particles
c^{\pm}
.
Next, we turn to the Poisson equation.
By the pointwise convergence (
56j
), we infer

\left(\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\cdot\nabla\psi_{\kappa}(t)\right)\boldsymbol{d}_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup^{*}(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi(t))\boldsymbol{d}\quad\quad\text{ in }L^{2}(\Omega)\text{ for all }t\in[0,T]\,.

Thus
(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)
fulfills the weak formulation (
3
)–(
5
) for all
t\in[0,T]
.
∎

Remark 4.13
.

By the uniqueness of the regularized solution it is straight forward to show the existence of a global weak solution to the regularized system.
One can then show the existence of a global weak solution to the original system by a standard diagonal sequence argument.

We next show that for our weak solutions a variant of the energy inequality for the regularized system from Proposition
4.8
transfers to the limit.

(Energy inequality for (
2
))
.

(73)

) with prefactor
\kappa
vanish,
we use Gagliardo–Nirenberg’s inequality
[

, Thm. 1.24]
,

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}\leq C_{\mathrm{GN}}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert^{1/2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert^{1/2}_{W^{2,2}(\Omega)}.

With that, we estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\Gamma}\kappa\partial_{t}\xi_{\kappa}\varphi_{\kappa}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}s\right|\leq{}

\displaystyle\kappa\left\lVert\partial_{t}\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{1}(\Gamma))}

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{trace\penalty\ estimate}}{\phantom{estimate}\leq}{}

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{GN}}{\leq}{}

\displaystyle{\kappa}C\left\lVert\partial_{t}\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T;L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}C_{\mathrm{GN}}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}^{1/2}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Omega))}^{1/2}

\vskip-5.69046pt
\lxSVG@closescope {{

and the elliptic estimate (
72b
) implies from
W^{1,2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow H^{1/2}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Gamma)\hookrightarrow L^{1}(\Gamma)
that

\displaystyle\left|\kappa\int_{\Gamma}\xi_{\kappa}(0)\varphi_{0\kappa}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\right|\leq{}

\displaystyle\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{0\kappa}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Gamma)}\leq C\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{0\kappa}\right\rVert_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{0\kappa}\right\rVert^{1/2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\varphi_{0\kappa}\right\rVert^{1/2}_{W^{2,2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\kappa^{1/2}\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\searrow 0.

For
s,p\in\mathbb{R}
with
p\in(1,2)
and
s>1/p
it holds
W^{s,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow W^{s-1/p,p}(\Gamma)
,

[
38
, Thm. 5.2]
,
where
W^{s,p}(\Omega)
and
W^{s-1/p,p}(\Gamma)
denote the Sobolev–Slobodeckij spaces,
see
[
38
, Def. 2.2 and Def. 2.3]
.
Additionally, we use a generalized Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality for Sobolev–Slobodeckij spaces
[
43
, Thm. II.3-3]
.
For
0\leq s_{1}<s_{2}<\infty
,
1<p_{1},p_{2}<\infty
,
\theta\in(0,1)
and

f\in W^{s_{1},p_{1}}(\Omega)\cap W^{s_{2},p_{2}}(\Omega)
it holds

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{W^{s,p}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{\theta}_{W^{s_{1},p_{1}}(\Omega)}\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{1-\theta}_{W^{s_{2},p_{2}}(\Omega)},

(74)

for
s=\theta s_{1}+(1-\theta)s_{2}
and
\frac{1}{p}=\frac{\theta}{p_{1}}+\frac{1-\theta}{p_{2}}
.
By the elliptic estimate (
72c
) with
q=5/4
and (
74
) with
\theta=1/4
,
s_{1}=0
,
p_{1}=2
,

s_{2}=1
and
p_{2}=5/4
, that is with
s=3/4
and
p=40/29
we obtain:

\displaystyle\left|\kappa\int_{\Gamma}\xi_{\kappa}(t)\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\right|\leq{}

\displaystyle\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Gamma)}\leq C\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{40/29}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert_{W^{3/4-29/40,40/29}(\Gamma)}

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{trace\penalty\ estimate}}{\phantom{estimate}\leq}{}

\displaystyle C\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert_{W^{3/4,40/29}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{interpolation\penalty\ }\eqref{eq:frac_interpol}}{\phantom{interpolati}\leq}{}

\displaystyle C\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert^{1/4}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert^{3/4}_{W^{1,5/4}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Young\penalty\ with\penalty\ }p=8}{\phantom{Youngwith}\leq}{}

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert^{8/7}_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert^{6/7}_{W^{1,5/4}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq:ellip_W1q_est}}{\phantom{M}\leq}{}

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\kappa\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert^{8/7}_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}\kappa^{-6/7}

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\varphi_{\kappa}(t)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\kappa^{1/7}\left\lVert\xi_{\kappa}\right\rVert^{8/7}_{C([0,T];L^{\infty}(\Gamma))}.

The first term can be absorbed into the left-hand side of (
71
)
and the other term vanishes for
\kappa\searrow 0
.

The only thing left to do is to argue that the convergences given by Proposition
4.11

are enough to pass to the limit in (
71
).
This follows from
the convergence (
56k
), which implies the convergence
\psi_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup\psi
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))

by the continuity and linearity of the trace operator.
Together with the strong convergence
\partial_{t}\xi_{\kappa}\to\partial_{t}\xi
in
L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))
we obtain the weak
convergence of the product
\partial_{t}\xi_{\kappa}\psi_{\kappa}\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}\xi\psi
in
L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}(\Gamma))
.
By the weak convergences (
56c
), (
56i
), (
56j
), (
56b
), (
56e
) and (
56l
)
and the weak lower semicontinuity of the norm and the convex function
a\mapsto a(\ln a+1)
, we can pass to the limit in (
71
) keeping the inequality sign
and thus we obtain (
70
).
∎

Putting the results of Proposition
4.11
and
4.14
together we can prove our main theorem.

Proof (of the Theorem
2.3
).

The limit from Proposition
4.11
already fulfills the weak formulation (
3
)–(
5
)
and the energy inequality (
6
) for all
t\in[0,T]
,
cf.
Proposition
4.14
.
The only thing left to prove for
(\boldsymbol{v},c^{\pm},\psi)
to be a weak solution according to Definition
2.2
is

\displaystyle c^{\pm}\in C_{w}([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega))\quad\text{ and }\quad\psi\in C_{w}([0,T];W^{1,2}(\Omega)).

We will see that
c^{\pm}\in C_{w}([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega))
by an application of
the de la Vallée–Poussin theorem,
[
36
, Thm. B.104]
.
We take an arbitrary
t\in[0,T]
and any sequence
(t_{n})_{n}\subseteq[0,T]
such that

t_{n}\to t
.
Now, we consider an arbitrary subsequence
(t_{n_{k}})_{k}
and get the uniform boundedness

\sup_{k}\left(\left\lVert c^{\pm}(t_{n_{k}})\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert c^{\pm}(t_{n_{k}})\ln(c^{\pm}(t_{n_{k}}))\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}\right)\leq C,

for some
C>0
independent of
n
and
k
,
by the energy inequality for the limit
cf.
Proposition
4.14
.
Thus, by the de la Vallée–Poussin theorem, we find a subsequence of
(t_{n_{k}})_{n}
which we will not relabel such
that
c^{\pm}(t_{n_{k}})\rightharpoonup c^{\pm}(t)
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
,
where we identified the limit with the help of
c^{\pm}\in C([0,T];W^{-1,10/9}(\Omega))
by (
56i
).
Since the subsequence
(t_{n_{k}})_{k}
was arbitrary we obtain the convergence
of the original sequence and thus
c^{\pm}(t_{n})\rightharpoonup c^{\pm}(t)
in
L^{1}(\Omega)
and we have proven
c^{\pm}\in C_{w}([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega))
.
By the boundedness of
(\psi(t_{n_{k}}))_{k}
in
W^{1,2}(\Omega)
from Proposition
4.14

and the reflexivity of
W^{1,2}(\Omega)
, we can extract a weakly convergent subsequence and we identify the limit with the help of the continuity of

c^{\pm}\in C_{w}([0,T];L^{1}(\Omega))
. Thus, our proof is complete.
∎

A
Appendix

A.1
Functions on the boundary

In this section we recall the definitions of Sobolev spaces on the boundary and state some important properties,
namely an integration by parts rule on the boundary and the relation of the tangential projection of a gradient of a function defined on the whole space
and the surface gradient of that functions restriction to the boundary.
We begin by recalling the definition of the regularity of a domain in dimension
d\in\mathbb{N}
with
d\geq 2
.
For that we need the notion of local coordinates, which are simply translated and rotated coordinates,
cf.
[
36
, below Def. 9.57]
.

Definition A.1
.

We call
T:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
rigid motion if it is given by
T(\boldsymbol{x})=\boldsymbol{c}+R(\boldsymbol{x})
for some
\boldsymbol{c}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
R:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
a
rotation, cf.
[
36
, Def. 9.23]
.
We then call
\boldsymbol{y}=T(\boldsymbol{x})
local coordinates.

We can now define the notion of regularity of a domain, where we follow
[
41
, Sec. 1.1.3]

and
[
36
, Def. 9.57]
.

Definition A.2
.

We call a bounded domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
of type
C^{k,1}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

and write
\Gamma=\partial\Omega\in C^{k,1}
, if there exist
\alpha,\beta>0
,
M\in\mathbb{N}
and local coordinates

\boldsymbol{y}_{r}=(y_{r1},\dots,y_{rd})=T_{r}(\boldsymbol{x})
for rigid motions
T_{r}
,
r=1,\dots,M
and functions

a_{r}:\Delta_{r}:=\left\{\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r}=(y_{r1},\dots,y_{r(d-1)})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\,\lvert\,|y_{ri}|<\alpha\text{ for }i=1,\dots,d-1\right\}\to\mathbb{R}

with
a\in C^{k,1}(\Delta_{r})

such that for all
\boldsymbol{p}\in\Gamma
there exists an
r
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r}\in\Delta_{r}
such that
T_{r}(\boldsymbol{p})=(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r},a(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r}))

and

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{y}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\lvert\,a_{r}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r})<y_{rd}<a_{r}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r})+\beta\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq T_{r}(\Omega)\text{ and }

\displaystyle\left\{\boldsymbol{y}_{r}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\lvert\,a_{r}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r})-\beta<y_{rd}<a_{r}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r})\right\}

\displaystyle\subseteq T_{r}\left(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\overline{\Omega}\right).

We now define
\varphi^{-1}_{r}:\Delta_{r}\to\Gamma
by
\varphi^{-1}_{r}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r})=T_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r},a(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r}))
.
This map is injective and of class
C^{k,1}
and thus has an inverse
\varphi_{r}:V_{r}\subseteq\Gamma\to\Delta_{r}
, where
V_{r}:=\varphi^{-1}(\Delta_{r})
.
We call
\varphi_{r}
local chart of
\Gamma
at
\boldsymbol{p}
and the set
\left\{(\varphi_{r},V_{r})\,\lvert\,r=1,\dots,M\right\}
atlas of
\Gamma
.
If
k=0
, that is if the
a_{r}
are only Lipschitz continuous, we call the domain
\Omega
Lipschitz.

Remark A.3
.

The function
\varphi^{-1}
from Definition
A.2
is even bi-Lipschitz continuous, since
for
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\in\Delta
we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\\
a(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\\
a(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})\end{array}\right)\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert^{2}+|a(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})-a(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})|}\geq\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\left\lVert\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\\
a(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})\end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\\
a(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})\end{array}\right)\right\rVert

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert^{2}+|a(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})-a(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})|}\leq(1+L)\left\lVert\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\right\rVert,

since
a
is Lipschitz.
The bi-Lipschitz continuity of
\varphi^{-1}
now follows from the fact that a rigid motion
T
, cf. Definition
A.1
,
preserves angles and distances.
From the bi-Lipschitz property of
\varphi^{-1}
the bi-Lipschitz property of
\varphi
follows from a straight forward calculation.

Next, we define
L^{p}-
spaces on the boundary,
cf.
[
41
, Sec. 2.4.1]
, where
p\in(1,\infty)
throughout this section and

p^{\prime}=p/(p-1)
its dual exponent.
For the remainder of this section we assume the domain to be Lipschitz unless explicitly stated otherwise.

Definition A.4
.

Let
\left\{(\varphi_{r},V_{r})\,\lvert\,r=1,\dots,M\right\}
be an atlas of
\Gamma
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The space
L^{p}(\Gamma)
is defined as the set of functions
f:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
defined almost everywhere
for which
f\circ\varphi_{r}^{-1}
is in
L^{p}(\Delta_{r})
for all
r=1,\dots,M
.

Remark A.5
.

Almost everywhere in
\Gamma
is to be understood with respect to the surface measure given by

\mu(\Sigma)=\int_{\varphi(\Sigma)}\sqrt{\det\left((D\varphi^{-1})^{T}D\varphi^{-1}\right)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r}

for
\Sigma\subseteq\varphi^{-1}(\Delta)

and generalized to arbitrary subsets of
\Gamma
by a partition of unity,
cf.
[
57
, Prop. B.4]
.

And similarly we define Sobolev space of the boundary,
cf.
[
41
, below Thm. 4.10]
.

Definition A.6
.

Let
\left\{(\varphi_{r},V_{r})\,\lvert\,r=1,\dots,M\right\}
be an atlas of
\Gamma
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The space
W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
is defined as the set of functions
f:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
defined almost everywhere,
such that
f\circ\varphi_{r}^{-1}
is in
W^{1,p}(\Delta_{r})
for all
r=1,\dots,M
.

Remark A.7
.

These definitions are independent of the choice of atlas, cf.
[
41
, Sec. 3.1, Lem. 1.1]
.
The proof is based on a coordinate transformation and the fact that if
u\in W^{1,p}(U)
and
\phi:U\to\tilde{U}
is a bi-Lipschitzian map
between two bounded open sets of
\mathbb{R}^{d}

than
u\circ\phi^{-1}\in W^{1,p}(\tilde{U})
,
cf.
[
15
, Cor. 3.46]
.

Following
[
41
, Sec. 2.4]
, we recall.

Lemma A.8

(
L^{p}(\Gamma)
is Banach)
.

Let
\left\{(\varphi_{r},V_{r})\,\lvert\,r=1,\dots,M\right\}
be an atlas of
\Gamma
and
\left\{\alpha_{r}\,\lvert\,r=1,\dots,M\right\}
a partition of unity
subordinate to the open cover

T^{-1}_{r}\left(\Delta_{r}\times\left\{y_{rd}\in\mathbb{R}\,\lvert\,\exists\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r}\in\Delta_{r}:|y_{rd}-a(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r})|<\beta\right\}\right)
of
\Gamma
.
Equipped with the norm
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Gamma)}
given by

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Gamma)}:=\left(\int_{\Gamma}|f|^{p}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}:=\left(\sum_{r=1}^{M}\int_{\Delta_{r}}|f\circ\varphi_{r}^{-1}|^{p}\;(\alpha_{r}\circ\varphi_{r}^{-1})\;\sqrt{\det((D\varphi_{r}^{-1})^{T}D\varphi_{r}^{-1})\;}\;\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

(A.1)

the space
L^{p}(\Gamma)
from Definition
A.4
is a Banach space.

Proof.

By
[
41
, Sec. 3.1, Lem. 1.3]
the definition of the norm in (
A.1
) is independent of the atlas and of the partition of unity.
The Banach property follows from
[
41
, Sec. 2.4, Thm. 4.1]
,
[
41
, Sec. 3.1, Lem. 1.1]

and
[
41
, Sec. 3.1, Lem. 1.2]
.
∎

We next would like to write the derivative of functions in
W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
more explicitly.
So we recall some definitions and results for local coordinate systems of manifolds.
First of all the partial derivatives of
\varphi_{r}^{-1}
form a basis for the tangent space,
[
3
, Rem. 10.5]
.

Lemma A.9
.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}\in\Gamma
and
\varphi
a local chart at
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\in\Delta
such that
\varphi(\boldsymbol{p})=\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}
.
Then the set

\left\{\tau_{i}=\partial_{i}\varphi^{-1}_{i}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})\,\lvert\,i=1,\dots,d-1\right\}

forms a basis for the tangent space of
\Gamma
in
\boldsymbol{p}
.

Next, we define the first fundamental matrix
g
.

Definition A.10
.

Let
\boldsymbol{p}\in\Gamma
and
\varphi
a local chart at
\boldsymbol{p}
and
\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\in\Delta
such that
\varphi(\boldsymbol{p})=\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}
.
We then define the first fundamental matrix
g
by

g=(g_{ij})^{d-1}_{i,j=1}=\left(\tau_{i}\cdot\tau_{j}\right)_{i,j=1}^{d-1}.

Remark A.11
.

The first fundamental matrix
g
is positive definite,
[
3
, Rem. 10.3e)]
.
Thus it is invertible. We will denote
g^{-1}=(g^{ij})_{i,j=1}^{d-1}
.
So the entries of the inverse matrix are also called
g
but with superscripts instead of subscripts.
This might be confusing at first but as this seems to be the common notation
we decided to use it as well.
We will denote
\tau^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}g^{ij}\tau_{j}
,
so again we are using superscripts instead of subscripts for the dual (“inverse”) basis.

We now define the surface gradient of a scalar function on the boundary.

Definition A.12
.

Let
f\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
. We define the surface gradient
\nabla_{\Gamma}f:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

at
\boldsymbol{p}\in\Gamma
with local chart
\varphi
at
\boldsymbol{p}
by

\nabla_{\Gamma}f=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}(f\circ\varphi^{-1})\;\tau^{i}\right)\circ\varphi,

where the derivative is to be understood in the weak sense.

Similarly we define the gradient of a vector valued function on the boundary.

Definition A.13
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)^{d}
we then define the surface gradient
\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

at
\boldsymbol{p}\in\Gamma
with local chart
\varphi
at
\boldsymbol{p}
by

\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})\otimes\tau^{i}\right)\circ\varphi,

where the derivative is to be understood in the weak sense.

Remark A.14
.

For these definitions to be meaningful they need to be independent of the chosen local chart.
For the gradient of a scalar valued function, cf. Definition
A.12
,
the independence of the local chart is proven in
[
57
, Prop. B.3]

and the proof for the vector valued functions works analogously
thus we omit it here.
Additionally, it is easy to see that we have
\nabla_{\Gamma}f\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
and
(\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
.

Additionally the surface gradient fulfills the following product rules.

Lemma A.15
.

For
\Gamma\in C^{1,1}
,
f\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
, and
g\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Gamma)
it holds

\nabla_{\Gamma}(fg)=g\nabla_{\Gamma}f+f\nabla_{\Gamma}g

and for two tangential vector fields
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{u}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Gamma)

it holds

\nabla_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})=(\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v})^{T}\boldsymbol{u}+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{u})^{T}\boldsymbol{v}.

(A.2)

Proof.

The product rule for scalar functions is a simple consequence of the standard product rule.
For the product rule for tangential vector fields we write

\boldsymbol{v}=\sum^{d-1}_{i=1}v_{i}\tau_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{u}=\sum^{d-1}_{i=1}u_{i}\tau_{i}
.
We first note

\displaystyle\partial_{j}(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})=\partial_{j}\left(\sum^{d-1}_{i=1}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\tau_{i}\right)=\sum^{d-1}_{i=1}\partial_{j}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\tau_{i}+(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{j}\tau_{i}.

With that we obtain

\partial_{j}((\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{u})\circ\varphi^{-1})=\sum^{d-1}_{i,l=1}\partial_{j}(g_{il}(v_{i}u_{l})\circ\varphi^{-1})\\
=\sum^{d-1}_{i,l=1}\left(g_{il}(u_{l}\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{j}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})+g_{il}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{j}(u_{l}\circ\varphi^{-1})+u_{l}v_{i}\partial_{j}\tau_{i}\cdot\tau_{l}+u_{l}v_{i}\partial_{j}\tau_{l}\cdot\tau_{i}\right)\\
=\partial_{j}(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})\cdot\boldsymbol{u}+\partial_{j}(\boldsymbol{u}\circ\varphi^{-1})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

and thus (
A.2
) follows.
∎

Now, that we have defined the surface gradient for scalar and vector valued functions living on the boundary
we would like to see how this definition relates to the (boundary) trace of the “bulk” gradient.
For that we come back to space dimension
d=3
for the remainder of this section.
It turns out that the surface gradient coincides with the tangential projection of “bulk” gradient.
We will make this more explicit in the following paragraph relying on results for the weak tangential trace published in
[
57
]
.
We start off by introducing the weak tangential trace,
cf.
[
57
, Def. 4.1]

Definition A.16

(Weak tangential trace)
.

We say that
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)
possess a weak tangential trace
\boldsymbol{q}
, if

\boldsymbol{q}\in L_{\tau}^{p}(\Gamma):=\left\{\boldsymbol{g}\in L^{p}(\Gamma)\,\lvert\,\boldsymbol{g}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0\text{ a.e. in }\Gamma\right\},

(where the almost everywhere is to be understood with respect to the measure on
\Gamma
)
and it holds

\int_{\Omega}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot(\nabla\times\boldsymbol{\Phi})-(\nabla\times\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{\Phi}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{q}\cdot(\boldsymbol{n}\times\boldsymbol{\Phi})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

(A.3)

for all smooth test function
\boldsymbol{\Phi}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and we write
\pi_{\tau}(\boldsymbol{v})=\boldsymbol{q}
.

Remark A.17
.

The tangential trace operator
\pi_{\tau}
is a well-defined operator from
W^{1,p}(\Omega)
to
L^{p}(\Gamma)
.
Defining
\boldsymbol{q}=S(\boldsymbol{v})-(S(\boldsymbol{v})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\in L^{p}(\Gamma)
since for
\Gamma\in C^{0,1}
we have
\boldsymbol{n}\in L^{\infty}(\Gamma)
,
we obtain the existence of a tangential trace for all
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)

and the uniqueness follows from the following observation.
Assuming there are two tangential traces
\boldsymbol{q}
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}}
we find

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{q}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{q}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{n}\times(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\times\boldsymbol{n})-(\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}})\boldsymbol{n}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{q}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{q}})\cdot(\boldsymbol{n}\times(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\times\boldsymbol{n}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

Since
\boldsymbol{n}\in L^{\infty}(\Gamma)
, we have
(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\times\boldsymbol{n})\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Gamma)
for
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\in L^{p^{\prime}}(\Gamma)
,
the right-hand side is zero by the definition of the tangential trace with
\boldsymbol{\Phi}=(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\times\boldsymbol{n})

using the density of
\{\boldsymbol{\Phi}|_{\Gamma}|\boldsymbol{\Phi}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\}
in
L^{p^{\prime}}(\Gamma)
,
[
38
, Prop. 2.8]
.

Via a detour to this weak tangential trace and the density of smooth functions in
W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
one gets the following result.

Theorem A.18
.

Let
f\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
.
Then the surface gradient is just the tangential projection of the bulk gradient, that is

\nabla_{\Gamma}S(f)=S(\nabla f)-(S(\nabla f)\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}

holds almost everywhere.

Proof.

Since we have
f\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
the trace
S(\nabla f)\in L^{p}(\Gamma)

and thus
S(\nabla f)-(S(\nabla f)\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\in L^{p}_{\tau}(\Gamma)
.
Using a
\mathrm{curl}-
integration by parts rule,
which is a simple consequence of Green’s Theorem, see
[
41
, Sec. 3.1, Thm. 1.1]
,
and the Lagrange identity for the vector cross product

(\boldsymbol{a}\times\boldsymbol{b})\cdot(\boldsymbol{c}\times\boldsymbol{d})=(\boldsymbol{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{c})(\boldsymbol{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})-(\boldsymbol{b}\cdot\boldsymbol{c})(\boldsymbol{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})

(A.4)

we find

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(\nabla f)

\displaystyle\cdot(\nabla\times\boldsymbol{\Phi})-(\nabla\times(\nabla f))\cdot\boldsymbol{\Phi}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle{=}\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\cdot\left(S(\nabla f)\times\boldsymbol{n}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\Phi}\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{n}\times S(\nabla f)\right)\underbrace{(\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})}_{=1}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}-\boldsymbol{\Phi}\cdot\left(\boldsymbol{n}\times S(\nabla f)\right)(\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})+\underbrace{\boldsymbol{n}\cdot(\boldsymbol{n}\times S(\nabla f))}_{=0}(\boldsymbol{\Phi}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle{=}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{n}\times\boldsymbol{\Phi})\cdot\left((\boldsymbol{n}\times S(\nabla f))\times\boldsymbol{n}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{n}\times\boldsymbol{\Phi})\cdot\left(S(\nabla f)-(S(\nabla f)\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

for all
\boldsymbol{\Phi}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
Thus we have
\pi_{\tau}(\nabla f)=\left(S(\nabla f)-(S(\nabla f)\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\right)

and using
[
57
, Thm. 4.2]

we find

\left(S(\nabla f)-(S(\nabla f)\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\right)=\pi_{\tau}(\nabla f)=\nabla_{\Gamma}(S(f)),

which finishes the proof.
∎

Next, we would like a similar result to Theorem
A.18
for vector valued functions.
This is basically just using the result for scalar valued functions and writing the vector field in the “right”
coordinate system, which unsurprisingly is the Euclidean system.
Nonetheless, we thought it important to do this short proof here.

Theorem A.19
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
. We then have

\nabla_{\Gamma}S(\boldsymbol{v})=S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n}.

Proof.

Writing
\boldsymbol{v}
in Euclidean coordinates we find
v_{i}\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
for
i=1,\dots,d
, such that

\boldsymbol{v}=\sum^{d}_{i=1}v_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
, where the
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
denote the standard basis vectors.
We find
S(\boldsymbol{v})=\sum^{d}_{i=1}S(v_{i})\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
by the linearity of the trace operator
S
.
The claim follows by the definition of the surface gradient for vector valued functions,
cf.
Definition
A.13
,
and Theorem
A.18
.
∎

Additionally, we define the divergence of a vector valued function on the boundary.

Definition A.20
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
we then define the surface divergence
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
by

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v}(\tau^{i}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{i}\circ\varphi).

One can again show that this definition is independent of the chosen chart,
which we will refrain from in order to keep things short.
We can again relate the surface divergence to the divergence in the bulk.

Theorem A.21
.

Let
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
. We then have

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot S(\boldsymbol{v})=S(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}.

(A.5)

Proof.

We again take the Euclidean representation of
\boldsymbol{v}
, that is
v_{i}\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
for
i=1,\dots,d
such that

\boldsymbol{v}=\sum^{d}_{i=1}v_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
, where the
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
denote the standard basis vectors.
Then by the definition of the surface divergence, Theorem
A.19

and the representations,

\boldsymbol{e}_{i}=(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}+\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\tau^{j})\tau^{j}=(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}+\sum_{l=1}^{d-1}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\tau_{l})\tau_{l}

(A.6)

we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}S(\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{v})&=\sum_{i=1}^{d}S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\\
&=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n})\right)\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\right)+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\right).\end{split}

(A.7)

The second term on the right-hand side is already the one we need, since we have

\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\right)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}(S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{n})_{i}\boldsymbol{n}_{i}\right)=(S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}.

The first term on the right-hand side of (
A.7
) becomes

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}

\displaystyle\left(S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n})\right)\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}_{i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\left(S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n})\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot(\tau^{j}\circ\varphi))(\tau^{j}\circ\varphi)\right)\cdot\left(\sum_{l=1}^{d-1}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot(\tau_{l}\circ\varphi))(\tau_{l}\circ\varphi)\right)
