\displaystyle=\sum_{j,l=1}^{d-1}\left(S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n})\right)(\tau^{j}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{l}\circ\varphi)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot(\tau^{j}\circ\varphi))(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\cdot(\tau_{l}\circ\varphi))\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,l=1}^{d-1}\left(S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n})\right)(\tau^{j}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{l}\circ\varphi)((\tau^{j}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{l}\circ\varphi))

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,l=1}^{d-1}\left(S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})-S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})(\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n})\right)(\tau^{j}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{l}\circ\varphi)\delta_{lj}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{d-1}\nabla_{\Gamma}S(\boldsymbol{v})(\tau^{l}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{l}\circ\varphi)=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot S(\boldsymbol{v}),

where we used that the normal parts of the basis vectors
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}
vanish due to the fact that the surface gradient is tangential.
Thus we get (
A.5
).
∎

Our next goal is to deduce an integration by parts rule on the boundary.
To that aim we first prove the following product rule.

Lemma A.22
.

For
\Gamma\in C^{1,1},

f\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Gamma)
it holds

\nabla_{\Gamma}(f\boldsymbol{v})=f\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}f\quad\text{ and }\quad\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(f\boldsymbol{v})=f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}f.

Proof.

We first calculate the surface gradient of
f\boldsymbol{v}
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}(f\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}\left((f\circ\varphi^{-1})(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})\right)\otimes\tau^{i}\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(\partial_{i}(f\circ\varphi^{-1})(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})+(f\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})\right)\otimes\tau^{i}\right)\circ\varphi=\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}f+f\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v},

where we used the product rule for weak derivatives in the bulk.
Next, by the definition of the surface divergence, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(f\boldsymbol{v})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\nabla_{\Gamma}(f\boldsymbol{v})(\tau^{i}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{i}\circ\varphi)=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(\boldsymbol{v}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}f+f\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v})(\tau^{i}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{i}\circ\varphi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(\nabla_{\Gamma}f\cdot(\tau^{i}\circ\varphi))(\tau_{i}\circ\varphi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+f\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d-1}\left(\partial_{j}(f\circ\varphi^{-1})(\tau^{j}\cdot\tau^{i})\tau_{i}\right)\circ\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+f\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d-1}\left(g^{ji}\partial_{j}(f\circ\varphi^{-1})\tau_{i}\right)\circ\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+f\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\left(\partial_{j}(f\circ\varphi^{-1})\tau^{j}\right)\circ\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+f\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\Gamma}f\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+f\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}.

∎

Next, we turn to the integration by parts rule.

Theorem A.23
.

Let
\Gamma\in C^{2,1}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
be a tangential vector field in
W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
,
that is we can write
\boldsymbol{v}
as

\boldsymbol{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}v_{i}\left(\tau_{i}\circ\varphi\right)

with
v_{i}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
.
It then holds

\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=0.

(A.8)

Remark A.24
.

In particular the above theorem does
not
hold for non tangential vector fields,
which can be seen by explicitly calculating the left-hand side for the domain
\Omega=B_{1}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}

and
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{n}=(x,y)^{T}
on
\Gamma
.

Proof.

The proof follows
[
48
, Sec. 2.1.6]
.
We first write the definition of the surface divergence in local coordinates
to derive the following formula of the surface divergence in local coordinates,

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(g)}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}\left(\sqrt{\det(g)}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\right)\right)\circ\varphi.

Then from the definition of the surface integral,
using an atlas for
\Gamma
and a subordinate partition of unity
(\alpha_{r})_{r=1,\dots,M}
we can use classical integration by parts in the bulk
to finish the proof.
We start by writing the surface divergence in local coordinates.
First of all we notice that

\displaystyle\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})=\partial_{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\tau_{j}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\left(\partial_{i}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\tau_{j}+(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{i}\tau_{j}\right).

With that, we can write the surface divergence as

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v}(\tau^{m}\circ\varphi)\cdot(\tau_{m}\circ\varphi)=\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}\left(\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})\otimes\tau^{i}\right)\;\tau^{m}\cdot\tau_{m}\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{m,i=1}^{d-1}\left(g^{im}\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})\cdot\tau_{m}\right)\circ\varphi=\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{v}\circ\varphi^{-1})\cdot\tau^{i}\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d-1}\left(\left(\partial_{i}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\delta_{ij}+(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})(\partial_{i}\tau_{j}\cdot\tau^{i})\right)\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\right)+\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{d-1}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})(\partial_{i}\tau_{j}\cdot\tau^{i})\right)\right)\circ\varphi.

Using
\partial_{i}\tau_{j}=\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\varphi^{-1}=\partial_{j}\partial_{i}\varphi^{-1}=\partial_{j}\tau_{i}

which follows from the fact that partial derivatives commute if they are continuous
(here we use that
\Gamma\in C^{2,1}
and thus
\varphi^{-1}\in C^{2,1}
),
we can further rewrite the second term on the right-hand side as

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{d-1}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})(\partial_{i}\tau_{j}\cdot\tau^{i})\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\left((v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(\partial_{j}\tau_{i}\cdot\tau^{i})\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\left(\sum_{k=1}^{d-1}(\partial_{j}\tau_{k}\cdot\tau^{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}(\partial_{j}\tau_{i}\cdot\tau^{i})\right)\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\left(\sum_{i,k=1}^{d-1}g^{ki}\left(\partial_{j}\tau_{k}\cdot\tau_{i}\right)+\sum_{i,k=1}^{d-1}g^{ik}\left(\partial_{j}\tau_{i}\cdot\tau_{k}\right)\right)\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle{=}\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\left((v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\sum_{i,k=1}^{d-1}\left(g^{ik}\left((\partial_{j}\tau_{k}\cdot\tau_{i})+(\partial_{j}\tau_{i}\cdot\tau_{k})\right)\right)\right)\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}\left((v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\sum_{i,k=1}^{d-1}\left(g^{ik}\partial_{j}g_{ki}\right)\right)\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\mathrm{tr}(g^{-1}\partial_{j}g)\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle{=}\frac{1}{2\det(g)}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d-1}(v_{j}\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{j}(\det(g))\right)\circ\varphi.

Putting these two expansions together we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(g)}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\left(\sqrt{\det(g)}\partial_{i}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\det(g)}}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{i}(\det(g))\right)\right)\circ\varphi

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(g)}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}\left(\sqrt{\det(g)}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\right)\right)\circ\varphi.

This finishes our prove of the surface divergence formula in local coordinates and we now turn to the proof of the integral identity (
A.8
).
Choosing a partition of unity
(\alpha_{r})_{r=1,\dots,M}
subordinate to our atlas domains.
That is we choose
(\alpha_{r})_{r=1,\dots,M}
subordinate to the open cover

\left(T_{r}^{-1}\left(\Delta_{r}\times\left\{\boldsymbol{y}_{rd}\in\mathbb{R}\,\lvert\,\exists\,\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r}\in\Delta_{r}:|\boldsymbol{y}_{rd}-a_{r}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}_{r})|<\beta\right\}\right)\right)_{r=1,\dots,M}=:\left(T_{r}^{-1}(O_{r})\right)_{r=1,\dots,M}

of
\Gamma
.
We then have by definition of the surface integral,
cf.
(
A.1
),
recalling that
\det(g)=\det((D\varphi^{-1})^{T}D\varphi^{-1})
,
cf.
Definition
A.10
,

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{M}\int_{\Gamma}\alpha_{r}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{M}\int_{\Delta_{r}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det(g)}}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}\left(\sqrt{\det(g)}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\right)(\alpha_{r}\circ\varphi^{-1})\sqrt{\det(g)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{r}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{r=1}^{M}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\int_{\Delta_{r}}\sqrt{\det(g)}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{i}(\alpha_{r}\circ\varphi^{-1})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{r}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\int_{\Delta_{r}}\sqrt{\det(g)}(v_{i}\circ\varphi^{-1})\partial_{i}(1)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}_{r}=0,

where we used the standard integration by parts rule and the fact that the partition of unity has compact support in
\Delta_{r}

so that no boundary terms appear and that the
\alpha_{r}
sum up to
1
.
To see that
\alpha_{r}\circ\varphi^{-1}
is indeed zero on the boundary of
\Delta_{r}
we use that for a rigid motion
T
we have

\partial(T(\Omega))=T(\partial\Omega)
for any set
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Thus for
\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}\in\partial\Delta_{r}
we have
(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime},a_{r}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}))\in\partial O_{r}

and thus
\varphi^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime})=T_{r}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime},a_{r}(\boldsymbol{y}^{\prime}))\in\partial(T_{r}^{-1}(O_{r}))
.
∎

Corollary A.25
.

For
\Gamma\in C^{2,1}
and a general (not necessarily tangential) vector field
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
and
f\in W^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Gamma)

the following integration by parts holds

\int_{\Gamma}f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-f(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}f\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=0.

The proof is taken from
[
48
, Sec. 2.1]
.

Proof.

This is a simple consequence of Theorem
A.23
.
For a possible non tangential vector field
\boldsymbol{v}
we can write
\boldsymbol{v}=(\boldsymbol{v}-(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n})+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}=:\boldsymbol{v}_{\tau}+(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}
.
The first part is a tangential vector field and thus by Theorem
A.23
and the product rule from Lemma
A.22

we have

\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}f\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}f\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\tau}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}_{\tau}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left((\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
=-\int_{\Gamma}f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-f\;(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}f\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-f\;(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,

where we used that the surface gradient is tangential, such that
\nabla_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
, see
[
57
]
.
∎

A.1.1
Matrix-valued functions on the boundary

All results from the previous section generalize to matrix-valued functions on the boundary.
We first define.

Definition A.26
.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}:\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be defined almost everywhere. Then
\boldsymbol{A}
is in
W^{1,p}(\Gamma)

if for an atlas
\left\{(\varphi_{r},V_{r})\,\lvert\,r=1,\dots,M\right\}
of
\Gamma
we have
\boldsymbol{A}\circ\varphi_{r}^{-1}\in W^{1,p}(\Delta_{r})

for all
r=1,\dots,M
.

As above we define the surface gradient.

Definition A.27
.

For
\boldsymbol{A}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
we define

\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{A}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\partial_{i}(\boldsymbol{A}\circ\varphi^{-1})\otimes\tau^{i}\right)\circ\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d\times d}.

Using this definition we also define the surface divergence.

Definition A.28
.

For
\boldsymbol{A}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
we define

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}=\sum_{m=1}^{d-1}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{A}\cdot(\tau^{m}\circ\varphi))(\tau_{m}\circ\varphi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

These definitions are all independent of the chosen atlas, which follows as before with the help of coordinate transformations.
Additionally, we get the following product rule.

Lemma A.29
.

For
\boldsymbol{A}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
it holds

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{v})=(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{T})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{A}^{T}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v}.

Proof.

Writing
\boldsymbol{A}=(a_{ij})_{i,j=1}^{d}
and
\boldsymbol{v}
in Euclidean coordinates, that is

\boldsymbol{A}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\otimes\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
and
\boldsymbol{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}v_{i}\boldsymbol{e}_{i}

the identity simply follows from the standard product rule and calculating both side explicitly.
∎

Corollary A.30
.

For
\Gamma\in C^{2,1}
and
\boldsymbol{A}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{1,p}(\Gamma)
it holds

\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}^{T}))\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{A}^{T}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v}-(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\,\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0.

(A.9)

Proof.

This is a very simple consequence of the product rule from Lemma
A.29
and the integration by parts rule from
Corollary
A.25
.
By Corollary
A.25
we find

\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{v})-(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=0

and the first term on the left-hand side can be rewritten as

\nabla_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{v})=(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}^{T})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+\boldsymbol{A}^{T}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{v}

and we are done.
∎

Now, we still would like to relate the surface divergence of a matrix-valued function on the boundary to the divergence of the bulk divergence.

Corollary A.31
.

For
\boldsymbol{A}\in W^{2,p}(\Omega)
we have

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot S(\boldsymbol{A})=\nabla\cdot\boldsymbol{A}-(\nabla\boldsymbol{A}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}.

Proof.

This simply follows from writing
\boldsymbol{A}
in Euclidean coordinates and using Theorem
A.21
.
∎

A.2
Auxiliary results for the flow model

Lemma A.32
.

For
\psi\in W^{4,10/3}(\Omega),\xi\in W^{3,10/3}(\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{d}\in W^{4,\infty}(\Omega)

fulfilling
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\psi=\xi
and
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
, it holds

\displaystyle\begin{split}(\varepsilon+\lambda)\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot&\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&=(\varepsilon+\lambda)\int_{\Omega}|\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}+\varepsilon|(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\mathrm{l.o.t}_{\kappa}\end{split}

(A.10)

for

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\frac{1}{(\varepsilon+\lambda)}\mathrm{l.o.t}_{\kappa}=\frac{1}{(\varepsilon+\lambda)}\left(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{l.o.t}_{\Omega\kappa}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}\mathrm{l.o.t}_{\Gamma\kappa}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\right)\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt:=\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\,\nabla\cdot\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Omega}\nabla^{3}\psi\text{\,\put(0.0,-2.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,0.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,2.0){$\cdot$}}\,\,\,\left((\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{d})\otimes_{M}\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla\boldsymbol{d}\right)^{T}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt-\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}))+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:\nabla^{2}\psi)(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}))\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:\nabla^{2}\psi)(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}+\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{d}):\left((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt-\int_{\Gamma}\left[(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\right]:\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\left[((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}))\boldsymbol{n}-(((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\right]\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt-\int_{\Gamma}\nabla^{2}_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi):((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})+\left(((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left((\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}):\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{n}+\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi):((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt-\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}-(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 20.00003pt+\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\left(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\right)\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})-\nabla_{\Gamma}\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\right)\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(A.11)

Proof.

Using

\displaystyle\nabla(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))=\nabla\cdot(\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))^{T}=\nabla\cdot\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\otimes\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\right)

and expanding the matrix
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})
on the left-hand side of (
A.10
) we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}&\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})+\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\,\nabla\cdot\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&=\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})+\varepsilon(\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\,\nabla\cdot\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}.\end{split}

(A.12)

The integral on the last line is no longer quadratic in the third order derivative of
\psi
and will be collected in

\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}
.
We now turn to the first term on the right-hand side of (
A.12
).
For the terms quadratic in the third order derivatives of
\psi
to occur with a good sign
we need to integrate by parts two times and handle the occurring boundary terms.
Using the integration by parts rule for matrices and tensors,
we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\Delta\psi)&\cdot\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi)\cdot\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&=-\int_{\Omega}(\nabla^{2}\psi):\nabla\cdot(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Omega}(\nabla^{3}\psi)\text{\,\put(0.0,-2.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,0.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,2.0){$\cdot$}}\,\,\,(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\Gamma}[(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}]:\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(A.13)

The first term gives a term with a good sign quadratic in the third order derivative of
\psi

and all other terms can be estimated and are part of
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}
.
That the first term on the right-hand side gives us terms with a good sign, can be seen by expanding the tensor product and using

\nabla(a\otimes b)=\nabla a\otimes_{M}b+a\otimes\nabla b

and
\nabla(Ab)=(\nabla A)^{T}\cdot b+A\nabla b
,
where for a matrix
A\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and a vector
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have
A\otimes_{M}b:=(a_{ik}b_{j})^{d}_{i,j,k=1}
and
b\otimes A:=(b_{i}a_{jk})^{d}_{i,j,k=1}
.
First we note

\displaystyle\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d})

\displaystyle=\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes_{M}\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla\boldsymbol{d}

\displaystyle=((\nabla^{3}\psi)^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\otimes_{M}\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{d})\otimes_{M}\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla\boldsymbol{d}

and then, using
(\nabla^{3}\psi)^{T}=\nabla^{3}\psi
,
where the transposed of a three dimensional tensor
\mathbb{G}=(g_{ijk})_{i,j,k=1}^{3}
is defined as

\mathbb{G}^{T}=(g_{ikj})_{i,j,k=1}^{3}
,
since we can interchange the order of derivatives,
(
\psi\in W^{4,10/3}(\Omega)

and thus
\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{x_{j}}\partial_{x_{k}}\psi\in W^{1,10/3}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow C(\overline{\Omega})
)
we get, using
(\boldsymbol{A}\otimes_{M}\boldsymbol{b})^{T}=\boldsymbol{A}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}
for
\boldsymbol{A}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3}
and
\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

and that for two tensors
\mathbb{G},\mathbb{B}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3\times 3}
it holds

\mathbb{G}^{T}\text{\,\put(0.0,-2.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,0.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,2.0){$\cdot$}}\,\,\,\mathbb{B}=\mathbb{G}\text{\,\put(0.0,-2.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,0.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,2.0){$\cdot$}}\,\,\,\mathbb{B}^{T}
,

\nabla^{3}\psi\text{\,\put(0.0,-2.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,0.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,2.0){$\cdot$}}\,\,\,((\nabla^{3}\psi)^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\otimes_{M}\boldsymbol{d})^{T}=\nabla^{3}\psi^{T}\text{\,\put(0.0,-2.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,0.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,2.0){$\cdot$}}\,\,\,((\nabla^{3}\psi)^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})=\nabla^{3}\psi\text{\,\put(0.0,-2.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,0.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,2.0){$\cdot$}}\,\,\,((\nabla^{3}\psi)^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\\
=\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{3}\left((\partial_{x_{k}}\partial_{x_{j}}\partial_{x_{i}}\psi)(\partial_{x_{l}}\partial_{x_{j}}\partial_{x_{i}}\psi)\boldsymbol{d}_{l}\boldsymbol{d}_{k}\right)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{3}(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})^{2}_{ij}=|\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}.

Using
\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{b})=\nabla\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}^{T})\cdot\boldsymbol{b}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{b}):\boldsymbol{A}^{T}

we can rewrite the second term on the right-hand side of (
A.12
) as

\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}(\nabla

\displaystyle(\Delta\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}\left[\nabla\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})-\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:\nabla^{2}\psi\right]\left[\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}))\boldsymbol{d})-\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\right]\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}))\boldsymbol{d})-\nabla\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:\nabla^{2}\psi)(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}))-(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:\nabla^{2}\psi)(\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}))\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}.

The first term also has to be integrated by parts two times to see that it has a good sign
and all other terms are in
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}
.
Performing the integration by parts we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla&\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}))\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
&=-\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\cdot(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})^{T})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\varepsilon\int_{\Omega}\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}):(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})^{T})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}-\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}((\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})^{T})\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(A.14)

The boundary integrals are in
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}
, which we will see below,
and the matrix scalar product gives a term with a good sign and lower order terms.
Using

\displaystyle(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})^{T}=(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}+(\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}

and

\displaystyle\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})^{T}=(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})^{T}+(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{d})^{T}=(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})+\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla^{2}\psi,

where we again used the fact that we can interchange the order of third order partial derivatives of
\psi
and thus have

\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}=(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})^{T}
,
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla(\nabla^{2}&\psi\boldsymbol{d}):(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\\
&=(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}+\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{d}):(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})^{T}=(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}):(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}+\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{d}):\left((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}\right)\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{d}:(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}.\end{split}

(A.15)

Using
\boldsymbol{a}\otimes\boldsymbol{b}:\boldsymbol{A}=\boldsymbol{a}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}b
we find for the first term on the right-hand side

\displaystyle(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}):(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}\otimes\boldsymbol{d}=|(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}|^{2}.

All other terms in (
A.15
) are of lower order, that is not quadratic in the third order derivative of
\psi
.
To see that also the boundary terms are of lower order, where we call boundary terms of lower order if there are no third order derivatives of
\psi
,
we need some involved integration by parts to be able to plug in the Robin boundary
condition for the electric potential
\psi
and reduce the order of derivatives by one.
For the first boundary integral in (
A.13
) we find
that the third order term in the second term vanishes since
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
and
for
\boldsymbol{A}\in C^{1}(\Omega)
and
\boldsymbol{b}\in C^{1}(\Omega)

\nabla(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{b})=(\nabla\boldsymbol{A})^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{A}\nabla\boldsymbol{b}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}&=\nabla(((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{n})-((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\\
&=\nabla((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})-((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\\
&=(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\\
&=(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\end{split}

(A.16)

and thus

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}\left[(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\right]:\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

Now, we turn to the second boundary integral in (
A.13
).
Using the characterization of the surface divergence of a matrix from Corollary
A.31
we find

\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
=\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot((\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

(A.17)

The higher order term in the second term again vanishes,
which can be seen by using
(\mathbb{G}\cdot\boldsymbol{a})\boldsymbol{b}=(\mathbb{G}^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{b})\boldsymbol{a}
,
for
\mathbb{G}\in\mathbb{R}^{3\times 3\times 3}
and
\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}

\displaystyle(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}=(\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}

and then proceeding as above in (
A.16
).
We now turn to the first term in (
A.17
). To see that we can reduce the order of derivatives by one
so that no terms with third order derivatives appear on the boundary we use the differential operators on the boundary

cf.
Theorem
A.18

and integration by parts on the boundary,
cf.
Corollary
A.25
.
We first note that for any test function
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\Gamma)
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma&=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(A.18)

Plugging this back into the first term of (
A.17
)
and using
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\boldsymbol{b})=(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{A})\cdot\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{A}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{b}
,

cf.
Lemma
A.29
,
and the integration by parts formular (
A.9
),
we obtain,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}&(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt-\int_{\Gamma}((\nabla\boldsymbol{n})^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}-\nabla^{2}_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi):((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})+\left(((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}))\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}):\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}((\nabla\boldsymbol{n})^{T}\nabla\psi):((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})-\left(((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,\end{split}

(A.19)

where the second term on the right-hand side vanishes due to
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
.
Using

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}))\boldsymbol{n}

\displaystyle=((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{d}=((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}=(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}-((\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}-((\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}-((\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}

we can reduce the last term in (
A.19
) including third order derivatives of
\psi

and we find

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})

\displaystyle\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}\nabla^{2}_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi):((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left((\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d}):\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{n}+\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi):((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle-\int_{\Gamma}\left(((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\;\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

Next, we turn to the boundary integrals in (
A.14
) and proceed as above.
For the first boundary integral on the right-hand side of (
A.14
)
we find as above that the third order term vanishes

\displaystyle\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})^{T}\boldsymbol{n}

\displaystyle=\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}))-(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}

\displaystyle=(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}

\displaystyle=(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}.

Next, we turn to the second boundary integral on the right-hand side of (
A.14
).
Using the characterization of the surface divergence,
cf.
Theorem
A.21
, we find

\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
=\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{n}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

The third order term in the second term on the right again vanishes as above.
For the first term we use (
A.18
) and integrate by parts

\displaystyle\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}))-\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}\left(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\right)\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d})-\nabla_{\Gamma}\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\right)\cdot((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{d})\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

Putting everything together we obtain (
A.10
).
∎

Lemma A.33
.

Under the assumptions from Lemma
A.32
and
\kappa>0
small enough,
that is for
\kappa
such that
C\kappa\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)\leq 1/32
,
we have

\kappa\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}\leq C+\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{1}{4}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

for some constant dependent on
\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}
and
\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}
and the outer normal field
\boldsymbol{n}
.

Proof.

We first turn to the volume terms of
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}
.
Using Hölder’s and Young’s inequalities we find

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega\kappa}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\right|\leq\kappa C\left\lVert\nabla^{3}\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\left(1+\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)+\kappa C\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle{\leq}\kappa C\left(\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+1\right)\left(1+\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)+\kappa C\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\kappa}{4}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\kappa C\left(1+\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\kappa}{4}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C,

where we used Lemma
A.35
and the smallness assumption on
\kappa
for the last inequality.
Now, we turn to the boundary integrals in
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}
,
cf.
(
A.11
).
For that we use that we can estimate the surface differential operators with the bulk one,

cf.
Lemma
A.36
below, to obtain

\displaystyle\kappa(\varepsilon

\displaystyle+\lambda)\left|-\int_{\Gamma}\left[(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\nabla(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{d})\otimes\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\right]:\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\right|

\displaystyle\leq\kappa C\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\kappa C\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{\kappa}{32}\left\lVert\nabla^{3}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq\kappa C\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{\kappa}{16}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C

\displaystyle\leq C+\frac{1}{32}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{\kappa}{16}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

where we used the trace estimate
[
17
, Prop. 8.2]
to find

\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\delta\left\lVert\nabla^{3}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+(1+\delta^{-1})\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

with
\delta=\frac{1}{32}\left(1+\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert_{W^{2,\infty}(\Omega)}\right)^{-1}
,
Lemma
A.35
to estimate

\left\lVert\nabla^{3}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq 2\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\left(\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+1\right)

and the smallness assumption for
\kappa
.
All other boundary terms in
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}
can be estimated similarly
and we obtain

\kappa\left|\int_{\Gamma}\mathrm{l.o.t}_{\Gamma\kappa}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\right|\leq\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{\kappa}{4}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C.

∎

Lemma A.34
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.9
,
we can estimate
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}
from Lemma
A.32
,

\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega}
given in (
47
),
and
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Gamma}
given in (
50
)
by:

\left|2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\left(\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Gamma}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+\kappa\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\right|\leq C+\frac{\tau}{2}\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}\\
+\tau\frac{\varepsilon+\lambda}{2}\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\frac{7}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}+\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}.

(A.20)

Proof.

We recall

\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega}=(\varepsilon+\lambda)\nabla^{2}\psi:\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\right)

from (
47
) and find

\left|2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Omega}\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\right|\leq 2(\varepsilon+\lambda)\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Omega}\left|\nabla^{2}\psi:\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{d}+\boldsymbol{d}\otimes(\nabla\boldsymbol{d}^{T}\nabla\psi)\right)\right|\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\\
\leq\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{4}_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}+C.

Next, we recall the definition of
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Gamma}
,
cf.
(
50
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Gamma}=-2\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi+\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\psi+(\xi-\tau\psi)^{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\xi-\tau\psi)\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+(\varepsilon+\lambda)\left((\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi-(\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})-(\xi-\tau\psi)(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\right).\end{split}

(A.21)

We estimate each term individually.
First, using Hölder’s inequality, we find

\displaystyle\left|2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}(-2\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\right|\leq C\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\frac{\tau}{2}\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}.

For the second term we use a trace estimate
and a Sobolev–Slobodeckij interpolation
[
43
, Thm. II.3-3]
, to estimate the gradient of
\psi
on the boundary by

\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1/4,2}(\Gamma)}\leq C\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{3/4,2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{7/4,2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{1/2}_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{3/2}_{W^{2,2}(\Omega)}.

With this estimate the second term in (
A.21
) can be estimated as follows,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hskip-10.00002pt\bigg|2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\bigg|&\leq C\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}\leq C\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{1/2}_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{3/2}_{W^{2,2}(\Omega)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\\
\underset{\mathrm{Young}}{}&{\leq}C\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{1,2}(\Omega))}+\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}.\end{split}

(A.22)

The last term on the right-hand side can be absorbed into the
|\nabla^{2}\psi|^{2}
term of (
53
)
with good sign and the first term is bounded by the first energy inequality.
For the third term in (
A.21
) we find

\displaystyle\left|2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)^{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\xi-\tau\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+C\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{1,2}(\Omega))}\right)\leq C.

The fourth term in (
A.21
) can be handled, using the trace estimate
[
17
, Prop. 8.2]
,
which gives us that for all
p\in[1,d)
there exists
C>0
such that for all
\delta>0
and all
u\in W^{1,p}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{q}(\Gamma)}\leq\delta\left\lVert\nabla u\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+C\left(1+\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

(A.23)

holds, for
q\in\left[1,\frac{(d-1)p}{(d-p)}\right]
.
With
q=2=p
, which is a valid choice for
d=3
, we find

\displaystyle\bigg|2\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}(-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n})^{T}\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}(\xi-\tau\psi))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{n}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\tau\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\left(\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\leq C+\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}.

For the fifth term in (
A.21
), which is the first on the second line,
we proceed analogously to (
A.22
) and find

\displaystyle\bigg|2(\varepsilon+\lambda)\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}(\boldsymbol{d}

\displaystyle\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{8}_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{1,2}(\Omega))}+\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}.

Next we turn to the second term on the second line of (
A.21
) and
find

\left|2(\varepsilon+\lambda)\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}(-(\nabla_{\Gamma}\xi\cdot\boldsymbol{d})(\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\right|\\
\leq C\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{1,2}(\Gamma))}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}+\tau\frac{\varepsilon+\lambda}{2}\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}.

Finally, we turn to the last term on the second line of (
A.21
)
and find

\displaystyle\bigg|2(\varepsilon+\lambda)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\int_{\Gamma}(-(\xi-\tau\psi)(\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\nabla\psi)\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t\bigg|

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\tau\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{2}_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}\int_{0}^{T_{\textrm{max}}}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{W^{2,2}(\Omega)}\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\tau\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}\right)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\lVert\boldsymbol{d}\right\rVert^{4}_{W^{1,\infty}(\Omega)}+1\right)\left(\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Gamma))}+\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};W^{1,2}(\Omega))}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\frac{1}{8}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}.

By Lemma
A.33
we have

\kappa\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\kappa}\leq C+\frac{\kappa}{2}\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}+\frac{1}{4}\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(0,T_{\textrm{max}};L^{2}(\Omega))}.

Putting this inequality and the estimates for
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Omega}
and
\mathrm{l.o.t.}_{\Gamma}

together we obtain (
A.20
).
∎

Lemma A.35
.

For
\psi\in W^{4,10/3}(\Omega),\xi\in W^{3,10/3}(\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{d}\in W^{4,\infty}(\Omega)

fulfilling
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+\tau\psi=\xi
and
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
, it holds

\left\lVert\nabla^{3}\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq 2\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\left(\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+1\right)

for some constant dependent on
\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{W^{1,2}(\Omega)}
.

Proof.

Assuming that
\psi\in W^{5,10/3}(\Omega)
the fourth order derivatives are continuous and we can interchange them
to obtain
\nabla\cdot(\nabla^{3}\psi)=\nabla(\nabla\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)
.
Using integration by parts two times, we find

\left\lVert\nabla^{3}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\int_{\Omega}\nabla^{3}\psi\text{\,\put(0.0,-2.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,0.0){$\cdot$}\put(0.0,2.0){$\cdot$}}\,\,\,\nabla^{3}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=-\int_{\Omega}\nabla^{2}\psi:\nabla\cdot\nabla^{3}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
=\int_{\Omega}(\nabla\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)\cdot(\nabla\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

(A.24)

By the density of smooth functions in
W^{4,10/30}(\Omega)
this also holds for
\psi\in W^{4,10/30}(\Omega)
.
The volume term is already the one we aimed for, since

\nabla\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi=\nabla\cdot(\nabla(\nabla\psi))^{T}=\nabla(\nabla\cdot\nabla\psi)=\nabla(\Delta\psi)

and we turn to the boundary integrals.
The first boundary term on the right-hand side can be rewritten using the surface divergence of a matrix,

cf.
Definition
A.28

and Corollary
A.31
:

-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
=-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

(A.25)

The last term is already of lower order, the second term will cancel with part of the other boundary term in (
A.24
)
and the first one has to be estimated.
For that we first note that for any test function
\boldsymbol{v}\in L^{2}(\Gamma)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\boldsymbol{v}\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.

Plugging this back in and using
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\boldsymbol{A}^{T}\boldsymbol{b})=(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{A})\cdot\boldsymbol{b}+\boldsymbol{A}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{b}
,

cf.
Lemma
A.29
, we obtain,

\displaystyle\begin{split}-{}&\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
={}&\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
={}&\int_{\Gamma}-\nabla^{2}_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi):\nabla^{2}\psi+\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\,\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&+\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi):\nabla^{2}\psi-(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\nabla^{2}\psi:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\,\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,\end{split}

(A.26)

where we used the integration by parts rule (
A.9
).
All terms except the third on the right-hand side are already of lower order and for this term we note

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}&(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n}))\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=-\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=-\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\Delta_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\,\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,\end{split}

(A.27)

where the second term after the second equality sign vanishes, since the surface gradient is tangential and thus
\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n}=0
on
\Gamma
.
Now we turn to the second boundary integral in (
A.24
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{3}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma&=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi-(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=\int_{\Gamma}\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi+(\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n}\otimes\boldsymbol{n}:\nabla^{2}\psi)-(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&=-\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi)-(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})-(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma.\end{split}

(A.28)

The first term on the last line indeed cancels with the second term in (
A.25
) and
the first term on the first line is identical to the first term on the right-hand side (
A.25
) and thus can be handled identical.
Putting (
A.26
), (
A.27
) and (
A.28
) back into (
A.25
),
where the terms from (
A.26
) and (
A.27
) now appear twice due to the term from (
A.28
), we can rewrite (
A.24
) as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla^{3}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}=\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-2\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\nabla^{2}\psi)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}|\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n}|^{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}-2\int_{\Gamma}\nabla^{2}_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi):\nabla^{2}\psi-\nabla_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt-2\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\nabla^{2}\psi:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{n})-\nabla_{\Gamma}(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi):\nabla^{2}\psi+(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+2\int_{\Gamma}\Delta_{\Gamma}(\xi-\tau\psi)(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})-\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})(\nabla\boldsymbol{n}^{T}\nabla\psi)\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}+(\nabla(\nabla\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})((\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot\boldsymbol{n})\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\int_{\Gamma}|\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n}|^{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{n}-(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{n}):\nabla^{2}\psi+(\nabla^{2}\psi\boldsymbol{n})\cdot(\nabla^{2}\psi\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\boldsymbol{n})\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+C\left\lVert\nabla\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+C

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\nabla(\Delta\psi)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\nabla^{3}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C\left\lVert\nabla^{2}\psi\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+C,

where we used the estimates from Lemma
A.36

and the trace embedding
[
17
, Prop. 8.2]
.
The term with the third order derivative can be absorbed into the left-hand side of (
A.24
) and thus our proof is complete.
∎

Lemma A.36
.

For
f\in W^{2,2}(\Omega)
,
\boldsymbol{v}\in W^{2,2}(\Omega)^{3}
and
g\in W^{3,2}(\Omega)
it holds

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}S(f)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\lVert S(\nabla f)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)},\quad\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}S(\boldsymbol{v})\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\lVert S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot S(\boldsymbol{v})\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\lVert S(\nabla\boldsymbol{v})\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)},

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla^{2}_{\Gamma}S(g)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\lVert S(\nabla^{2}g)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+C\left\lVert S(\nabla g)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Delta_{\Gamma}S(g)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}\leq\left\lVert S(\nabla^{2}g)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+C\left\lVert S(\nabla g)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}

where the constant
C>0
depends on
\left\lVert\nabla\boldsymbol{n}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma)}
.

Proof.

Using the characterization of the surface derivatives by the projection of the bulk derivatives,

cf.
Theorem
A.18
, Theorem
A.19

and Theorem
A.21
this follows from straight forward calculations.
∎

A.3
Some additional proofs for the interested reader

Proof (of Lemma
3.1
).

By the Hille–Yosida generation theorem we know that
(0,\infty)
is in the Resolvent set
\rho(A)
of
A
,
see for example
[
22
, Thm. 3.5]
, and the Resolvent

R(\lambda,A):=(\lambda-A)^{-1}:X\to D(A)\subseteq X
is well-defined for all
\lambda\in\rho(A)
and in
\mathcal{L}(X)
.
Thus the well-definedness of
R_{\kappa}
follows from

R_{\kappa}=(I-\kappa A)^{-1}=\left(\kappa\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}-A\right)\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{\kappa}R(1/\kappa,A).

We now prove the continuity from item
1
.
For that we use
[
44
, Lem. 3.2]
, by which we have
\lim_{\lambda\to\infty}\lambda R(\lambda,A)x=x
for all
x\in X

and the uniform bound
\left\lVert R(\lambda,A)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\leq M/\lambda
for some
M>0
,
which also comes from the Hille–Yosida generation theorem.
With these tools, we can estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x_{\kappa})-x\right\rVert_{X}&\leq\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x_{\kappa}-x)\right\rVert_{X}+\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x)-x\right\rVert_{X}\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\lVert R(1/\kappa,A)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(X)}\left\lVert x_{\kappa}-x\right\rVert_{X}+\left\lVert\frac{1}{\kappa}R(1/\kappa,A)(x)-x\right\rVert_{X}\\
&\leq M\left\lVert x_{\kappa}-x\right\rVert_{X}+\left\lVert\frac{1}{\kappa}R(1/\kappa,A)(x)-x\right\rVert_{X},\end{split}

by the above mentioned bound on the operator norm of the Resolvent.
The right-hand side goes to zero, by the strong convergence of
\{x_{\kappa}\}
and the continuity result from
[
44
, Lem. 3.2]

mentioned above.
We next prove item
2
. For arbitrary
\varphi\in X^{*}
we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\varphi,\,R_{\kappa}(x_{\kappa})-x\right\rangle=\left\langle\varphi,\,R_{\kappa}(x_{\kappa})-x_{\kappa}\right\rangle+\left\langle\varphi,\,x_{\kappa}-x\right\rangle.

The second term on the right-hand side goes to zero for
\kappa\to 0
by the definition of weak convergence
and for the first term we observe

\displaystyle|\left\langle\varphi,\,R_{\kappa}(x_{\kappa})-x_{\kappa}\right\rangle|

\displaystyle=\left|\left\langle\varphi,\,\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}R(1/\kappa,A)-I\right)x_{\kappa}\right\rangle\right|\leq\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{X^{*}}\left\lVert x_{\kappa}\right\rVert_{X}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\kappa}R(1/\kappa,A)-I\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(X)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{X^{*}}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\kappa}R(1/\kappa,A)-\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}-A\right)R(1/\kappa,A)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(X)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{X^{*}}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\kappa}-\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}-A\right)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(D(A),X)}\left\lVert R(1/\kappa,A)\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(X)}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{X^{*}}\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(D(A),X)}\kappa,

where we used that weakly convergent sequences are bounded
and the uniform bound on the operator norm of the Resolvent given by the Hille–Yosida theorem.
The right-hand side goes to zero for
\kappa\searrow 0
and the weak convergence of
(R_{\kappa}(x_{\kappa}))
to
x
follows.
Item
3
is again a simple consequence of this uniform bound.
Item
4
can be deduce by a simple estimation using
AR(\lambda,A)=\lambda R(\lambda,A)-I
,
see for example
[
22
, Sec. 1, Chap. IV]
.
For all
x\in X
we have,

\displaystyle\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x)\right\rVert_{Y}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x)\right\rVert_{D(A)}=C\left(\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x)\right\rVert_{X}+\left\lVert A(R_{\kappa}(x))\right\rVert_{X}\right)

\displaystyle=C\left(\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x)\right\rVert_{X}+\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\lVert A(R(1/\kappa,A)(x))\right\rVert_{X}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{X}+\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\kappa}R(1/\kappa,A)(x)\right\rVert_{X}+\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{X}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(1+\frac{1}{\kappa}\right)\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{X}+C\frac{1}{\kappa}\left\lVert R_{\kappa}(x)\right\rVert_{X}\leq C(1+1/\kappa)\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{X}

for
C>
independent of
\kappa
,
where we used the estimate from item
3
for the first and the last inequality.
∎

Proof (of Lemma
4.5
).

By the non-negativity of
c^{\pm}
and mass conservation we obtain

\left\lVert c^{\pm}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))}=\left\lVert c^{\pm}_{0}\right\rVert_{L^{1}(\Omega)}

and thus by Corollary
3.8
and the Sobolev embedding
W^{1,6/5}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\Omega)

we obtain

\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq C\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,6/5}(\Omega))}\leq\frac{C}{\kappa}\;\hbox to55.88pt{\vbox to17.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.70775pt\lower-8.32655pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

(A.29)

Testing (
8b
) with
\psi
we obtain

\int_{\Omega}|\nabla\psi|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}(\boldsymbol{d})}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\tau\int_{\Gamma}|\psi|^{2}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma=\int_{\Omega}\varphi\psi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}+\int_{\Gamma}\psi\xi\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\sigma,

applying Young’s inequality we can estimate

\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}\leq C\left(\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Gamma))}\right).

Combining this estimate with Agmon–Douglis–Nirenberg elliptic estimates, see
[
40
, Thm. 3.1.1]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Omega))}&\leq C\left(\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\left\lVert E(\xi)\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))}\right)\\
&\leq C\left(\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Gamma))}\right)\\
&\leq\frac{C}{\kappa}\;\hbox to55.88pt{\vbox to17.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 24.70775pt\lower-8.32655pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-21.46248pt}{-1.95834pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -29.7 -2.71)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{

(A.30)

where
E
denotes the trace extension operator.
Next, we test (
8a
) with
c^{\pm}
. By an integration by parts, see
[
20
, Cor. 8.1.10]
, we obtain

\frac{\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}}{\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}t}\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert c^{\pm}(t)\right\rVert^{2}_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\int_{\Omega}|\nabla c^{\pm}(t)|^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})}\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}=-\int_{\Omega}c^{\pm}(t)\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\nabla\psi(t)\cdot\nabla c^{\pm}(t)\mathrm{\hskip 2.0ptd}\boldsymbol{x}\\
\leq\left\lVert c^{\pm}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{3}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\boldsymbol{\Lambda}(\boldsymbol{d})\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\nabla\psi(t)\right\rVert_{L^{6}(\Omega)}\left\lVert\nabla c^{\pm}(t)\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

(A.31)

for almost all
t\in(0,T)
, where we used that
\boldsymbol{v}
is divergence free and Hölder’s inequality.
Using Gagliardo–Nirenberg’s inequality and a classical Gronwall argument we
obtain the bound of
c^{\pm}
in

L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;W^{1,2}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega))\cap L^{2}(0,T;L^{6}(\Omega))\hookrightarrow L^{3}(0,T;L^{3}(\Omega))

and thus the boundedness of the first two terms in (
10
) follows.
For the third term in (
10
) we note that
by elliptic regularity
[
40
, Thm. 3.1.1]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Omega))}\leq C\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\;\hbox to89.24pt{\vbox to17.97pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.38545pt\lower-8.32655pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

and thus by higher order elliptic estimates
[
31
, Rem. 2.5.1.2]
, and (
A.30
) we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{4,2}(\Omega))}\leq C\left(\left\lVert\varphi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{2,2}(\Omega))}+\left\lVert\xi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(0,T;W^{3,2}(\Gamma))}\right)\leq C(\kappa),

which finishes the proof of Lemma
4.5
.
